Romans 5:15b For if by the trespass of one many died, much more did the grace of God and the gift in the grace of one man, Jesus Christ, overflow to many. 

That is, if by the trespass of one many died by imitating his transgression, much more did the grace of God and the gift overflow to many who flee to him. For more obtain grace than died by Adam’s trespass. From this it is clear that he did not mean this death which is common to all; because all without exception die, and yet not all obtain grace. Nor is it said that death reigned over all, but over those who died through Adam’s trespass, those whom he says sinned in the likeness of Adam’s transgression. These are those whom he calls “many dead through the trespass of one,” but the grace of God overflowed to more; because in those who are said to have died by Adam’s trespass, sinning in like manner, and in those who did not sin in this likeness of Adam’s transgression, the grace of God overflowed. And though by the father’s sin, according to God’s judgment, they were in the underworld (inferos), the grace of God overflowed in the descent of the Savior, granting pardon to all, and, with triumph, lifting them up into heaven. (1 Peter 3:18-20)

Romans 5:16 And not as with the one sin, so also is the gift: for the judgment indeed came from one unto condemnation, but the gift from many offenses unto justification. 

It is clearly different, because by the one sin of Adam those who sinned in the likeness of his transgression were condemned; but the grace of God through Christ has justified human beings not from one offense, but from many, by granting them the remission of sins. This is spoken to the greatness of the mercy of God and of Christ; so that, although through Adam’s offense many are held by the second death in the lower hell (inferno), yet the gift of God’s grace, when it would have been just for them to be punished, not only pardons them, but justifies them.

Romans 5:17 For if by the offense of one man death reigned through one, much more shall those who receive the abundance of grace and of the gift and of righteousness reign in life through the one Jesus Christ. 

It must be noted that there is one and the same meaning, and that in no way does it differ: for Paul says that death has reigned, not that it is reigning; because those who understood God’s judgment through the Law have been lifted out from under its dominion. But it did reign, because without the revealing of the Law, the fear of God was not upon the earth. The earlier sense, therefore, is that because death reigned from Adam to Moses over those who sinned in the likeness of Adam’s transgression, much more does grace reign, in the abundance of God’s gift, unto life through the one Jesus Christ. For if death reigned, why should not grace reign much more, which justifies far more people than those in whom death reigned? How much more must grace be believed to reign, which bestows life through Christ?

Romans 5:18 Therefore, just as through the transgression of one man condemnation came upon all men, so also through the righteousness of one man justification of life came upon all men:

That is, just as through the transgression of one man all deserved condemnation by sinning in like manner, so also in the righteousness of one man all who believe will be justified. But if some think that this condemnation is universal, in the same way they will take the justification to be universal. Yet this is not true, because not all believe.

Romans 5:19 For just as through the disobedience of one man many were made sinners, so through the obedience of one man many will be made righteous. 

Those whom Paul previously called “all,” he here designates as “many”; for more people followed Adam’s offense by transgression, but not all; and many will be made righteous through faith in Christ, but not all. Therefore death did not reign over those who did not sin in the likeness of Adam’s transgression.

Romans 5:20a But the Law came in so that the offense might increase.

One might say conversely: Therefore the Law ought not to have been given, lest sins grow; for if before the Law there was less sin, there was no need of the Law. The Law was indeed necessary, so that it might show that sins are counted before God, which were believed to be committed with immunity; so that thereafter people would know what they ought to avoid. Therefore the prophet Isaiah says: “The Law,” he says, “was given as a help” (Isa. 8:20); because certain seeds of justice are in some manner implanted in nature itself, the Law was added so that, by its authority and teaching, the natural disposition might advance to produce the fruit of righteousness. For just as birth perishes unless it has nourishment by which, being sustained, it may grow, so also the natural disposition toward justice, unless it has something to look up to and venerate, does not easily flourish, but falls sick and yields to sins when they come. For by the habit of sinning it is pressed down so that it does not grow into fruit, and thereby it is extinguished. Therefore the Law was providentially given as a help, as the prophet bears witness; but the people, following their old habit, multiplied sins. For by practice they began to sin more than they had sinned before; and therefore it came to pass that, when the Law was given, sins were not diminished but increased. The Apostle shows what resulted from the giving of the Law, not what the Law itself did. But how was it by fault of the Law that sins increased, when the Law threatened punishment so that people might not sin; and yet it is said to have come in so that sin might abound? It is true, because it entered, as it were, as something that would be helpful, and with humility; but afterwards it began to rule over those to whom it had commanded not to sin, and they did sin. For the Law began to reveal the abundance of sins, since the very things it had forbidden were done more than they had been done before. Therefore, in this way the Law was given so that the offense might increase; for as the Apostle praises faith, through which sins are indeed wiped out, he said that the Law caused sin to abound, as I said above; because it was not given so that sins might be abolished, but so that they might abound: since it showed people to be sinners before the Law, and after the Law it held all guilty.

Romans 5:20b But where sin abounded, grace abounded all the more. 

It is clear that when sin abounded, grace abounded all the more: when the gift of God came from the promise and covered the sins of all, so that the Devil might grieve that through his envy he had accomplished nothing. For when the Law had been given for human benefit, the Devil worked to overturn it by persuading people to unlawful deeds, so that what had been given to be helpful would in fact work in the opposite way, since the commandments were despised, and from the Law there proceeded not benefit but judgment unto vengeance. Then the just and merciful God, in order to empty the glory of the Devil which he had sought from his triumph over man, decreed that His Son should come, who would forgive all sins, so that the joy arising from the gift of grace might be greater than the sorrow that had arisen from sin. For the joy of God’s gift also benefits those over whom Satan could not triumph; therefore grace abounded beyond the sin which Satan had prompted.

Romans 5:21 As sin reigned unto death, so also let grace reign through righteousness unto eternal life through Jesus Christ our Lord. 

Sin reigned as it saw its work in sinners advancing toward death, in which it gloried; in like manner let grace also reign through righteousness unto eternal life, through Jesus Christ our Lord, so that just as, through Adam, sin began to reign, so also, through Christ, grace. And grace reigns through righteousness if, having received the forgiveness of sins, we follow after righteousness; so that, seeing that it has fruit in the good whom it has redeemed, grace may reign unto eternal life, knowing that we shall be eternal. Grace therefore abounds, because sin reigned for a time, but grace forever. For it is God’s kingdom when grace reigns, just as it was the Devil’s kingdom when sin reigned. Yet he refers everything to Christ, so that all the grace of God may be learned from Christ. He therefore answers according to the foregoing sense, saying:

Chapter 6

Romans 6:1 What then shall we say? Shall we remain in sin so that grace may abound? Far from it. 

That is, must we always keep sinning so that the gift of God may abound, covering our sins; so that, always hoping for the forgiveness of sins because God is merciful, we might put forward the grace of Christ? Certainly not. For this is precisely why God took pity on us through Christ: so that, from now on, by not sinning, we might cause both merit for ourselves and the grace of God to reign in us. For whoever returns again to the old man, that is, to the pattern of former life, removes the reign of God’s grace and hands himself over to sin. For we have received mercy for two reasons: both that the kingdom of the Devil might be driven out, and that God’s rule might be proclaimed to those who are ignorant, and by this very thing a dignity might be obtained for us.

Romans 6:2 For we who have died to sin, how shall we still live in it? 

Paul says this because, while we were living in sin, we were dead before God; for whoever sins lives to sin, just as whoever does not sin lives to God. For when the grace of God came through Christ, and we, through faith and the regenerating spiritual washing, began to live to God, we became dead to sin, which is the Devil; that is, to die to sin is to be freed from sin and to become a servant of God. Therefore, since we are now dead to sin, let us not return to our former evils, lest we again live to sin and, dying to God, lose our dignity and fall back into the punishment we had escaped.

Romans 6:3 Are you unaware that all of us who have been baptized into Christ Jesus have been baptized into his death? 

Paul says this so that we may understand that, having been baptized, we ought no longer to sin; for when we are baptized, we die together with Christ. This is what it means to be baptized into his death; for there all our sins die, so that, renewed with death laid aside, we may be seen to rise again to life reborn: so that just as Christ died to sin and rose again, so we too, through baptism, may have the hope of resurrection. Baptism, therefore, is the death of sin, so that another birth may follow, which, with the structure of the body remaining, renews a person in mind, with the oldness of all evil deeds buried.

Romans 6:4 For we have been buried together with him through baptism into death; so that just as Christ rose from the dead through the power of the Father, so we too may walk in newness of life. 

In saying this, Paul also signifies that which is primary: because Christ raised his own body from the dead, for he himself is the power of God the Father, as he says: Destroy this temple, and I will raise it up in three days. But, he says, he was speaking of the temple of his body (Jn. 2:19 and 21); and because he rose into a new life which no longer knows death. It is called new because it is given by Christ, that is, the Christian way of life. We, however, who have been baptized, have been buried with Christ (Col. 2:12) so that from then on we may follow that life in which Christ rose. Baptism, therefore, is the pledge and image of the resurrection; so that, remaining in the commandments of Christ, we do not again turn back to what is past. For the one who dies no longer sins; for death is the end of sin. Indeed, it is performed with water so that, just as water washes away the stains of the body, so we may believe that through baptism we are spiritually cleansed and renewed from every sin; so that what is incorporeal may be washed away invisibly.

Romans 6:5 For if we have been planted together in the likeness of his death, we shall also be in that of his resurrection. 

Then Paul says that we can happily rise again, if we have been planted together in the likeness of his death; that is, if in baptism, having laid aside all vices and been transferred into a new life, we no longer sin thereafter: by this we shall likewise be similar to his resurrection; for the likeness of the death will grant a similar resurrection. This is what the apostle John recalls in his letter, saying: We know that when he appears, we shall be like him (1 Jn. 3:2), that is, to rise immortal and glorious. For this will not be a likeness such that there is no difference at all, since it will be likeness in the glory of the body, not in the nature of the divinity.

Romans 6:6-7 Knowing this, because our old man was crucified together with Him, so that the body of sin might be destroyed, so that we may no longer serve sin. 

Paul weaves together and repeats many things in order to teach the baptized that they ought no longer to sin, especially that they should not return to idolatry; for it is a most grave crime and the chief of all error, lest they lose the grace received from God through Christ. By “old man” he means past deeds, because just as a new man is so called through faith and a pure life, so the old man is so called through lack of faith and evil deeds. He says these deeds are crucified, that is, put to death, so that the body of sin, which is all crimes together, may be destroyed. For he calls all sins together a body, which he says is destroyed through a good life and the Catholic faith.

Romans 6:8 But if we have died with Christ, we believe that we shall also live together with Him.

It is clear that those who have crucified the flesh, that is, the world with its vices and desires, die to the world and die together with Christ, and are also mystically conformed to eternal life and salvation, so that they may deserve to be made like to the glory of Christ. Now the flesh, that is, the body, is crucified in this way if its desires are trampled underfoot, which, with sin remaining in it, arise from the transgression begun by the first man: for in the flesh the Devil is crucified; for he it is who deceives through the flesh. Therefore sometimes “flesh” means the world, that is, the elements, and sometimes it is understood as the human body, or even the soul itself following bodily vices.

Romans 6:9-10 Knowing that Christ, rising again from the dead, now no longer dies; death will no longer have dominion over him. For the death he died, he died to sin once; but the life he lives, he lives to God.

Paul shows the security of eternity in the resurrection of the Savior, which it is possible to attain if there is striving after a better life. For whoever, by acting rightly, lives to God, truly lives; because he has eternal life.

Romans 6:11 So you also must consider yourselves as indeed dead to sin, but living to God in Christ Jesus our Lord. 

Paul speaks therefore to teach that there is no other way to die to sin and live to God than for all hope to be in Christ; whom he calls our Lord, so that nothing in the Law may be thought to contribute to the attaining of salvation in the age to come, when one can live to God only through Christ.

Romans 6:12 Therefore, let not sin reign in your mortal body, so that you obey its desires. 

The body is mortal because of Adam’s transgression, but by faith in Christ it is believed that it will become immortal. Yet, in order to attain this promise, Paul says that we must not obey sin, lest it reign in our mortal body; for it reigns when it commands. But if it does not reign, this body will not be seen as mortal, because it lives in the hope of eternity. He did not call the body mortal on account of bodily termination, but because of the punishment of Gehenna [gehennæ]; so that the one is called mortal who is to be sent into Gehenna. For those who obey sin do not escape the second death of hell [inferni], from which the Savior has freed those who believe in him. By calling the body mortal, he meant the whole person; for those who obey sin are called mortal. “The soul,” he says, “that sins, it shall die” (Ezek. 18:4), that is, the whole man, for no one will be judged without the body.

Romans 6:13a But do not present your members as weapons of unrighteousness to sin. 

Paul shows that the Devil attacks us with his darts; for an opportunity is given him through our sins, so that when God abandons us, the Devil, having received power, mocks us and puts us to death. Therefore we must restrain our members from every work of iniquity, so that our enemy, found unarmed, may be subdued. And he did not say: “Present your bodies,” but “your members”; for it is through the members, as servants, that one goes astray, not through the whole body.

Romans 6:13b But present yourselves to God as those alive from the dead. 

By “dead” Paul means ignorance and unbelief together with an evil life; for life is to know God through Christ. Since no one receives the origin of life without a parent, and all things have their origin from God unto life through Christ, therefore the one who does not know that God through Christ is the parent of all is not said to have life, that is, he is regarded as if he did not exist: for he denies his own being when he imagines that he exists without God as his parent. Therefore ignorance and shameful living are death; such vices bring upon a person death, not this death which is common to all, but the death of Gehenna [gehennae], as I mentioned above; so that the knowledge of God as Father and holy conduct are life, not this life which is subject to decay, but that of the age to come, which is called eternal. Therefore, “present yourselves to God,” he says, “through knowing whom you have advanced unto salvation; so that, renouncing a dishonorable life, you may be alive from the dead.”

Romans 6:13c And let your members be weapons of righteousness for God. 

Such modesty does He wish to govern our conduct with, that our actions, advancing the righteousness of God and not worldly righteousness (for the world’s righteousness, being without faith in Christ, without which there is not life but death), supply Him with weapons to defend us. When we offer Him weapons through good works, we make ourselves worthy to be helped by it; for the righteousness of God looks down on the unworthy. For where the righteousness of God is, there the Holy Spirit dwells to help our weakness. Just as we provide weapons to sin when we act wickedly, so also to righteousness when we live rightly, guarding our members from all shamefulness.

Romans 6:14 But sin will no longer rule over you; for you are not under the Law, but under grace. 

If we walk according to the commands that he gives, he declares that sin cannot rule over us; for it rules over those who sin. Indeed, when we walk as he commands, we are not under the Law; but if we do not sin, we are not under the Law, but under grace. If, however, we sin, we come under the Law, and sin will begin to rule over us; for whoever commits sin is the slave of sin (Jn. 8:34); and it is necessary that he be under the Law as long as he has not received forgiveness; for sin, by the authority of the Law, makes the sinner guilty. And therefore, whoever has been granted forgiveness and keeps himself so that he does not sin thereafter, neither will sin rule over him, nor is he under the Law. For the authority of the Law ceases with respect to the one who has been freed from sin; for those whom the Law holds as guilty have been handed over to it by sin. Therefore, the one who is stripped of sins cannot be under the Law.

Romans 6:15 What then? Shall we sin because we are not under the Law, but under grace? God forbid.

Since the Law is from God, and so that it might not somehow seem opposed to Him, Paul sets the question before himself: “If the Law is from God,” he says, “why should we not be under the Law?” This he clarifies, and teaches that in accordance with the will of God, who is the author of the Law, we have been freed by Christ from being under the Law. For although the Law was rightly given (it was given so that it might show as guilty before God those who had sinned before the Law, and so that it might inspire fear, that from this time people might not sin), yet because the human race, through the weakness of its frailty, could not restrain itself from sins, it became liable to the death of hell [inferni]. Moved by the compassion of His mercy, by which He always comes to the aid of the human race, God provided through Christ a way by which healing might be given to those already in despair; so that, having received the forgiveness of sins, being delivered from the Law which held them liable, and being lifted up and restored, they might, with God’s help through faith, resist the vices by which they had previously been subdued. And therefore we have not sinned as though scorning God’s Law, but have rather followed, through Christ, the very providence of God Himself.

Romans 6:16 You do not know that to whom you present yourselves as servants to obey, you are servants of the one whom you obey, whether of sin unto death or of obedience unto righteousness. 

Now, lest we profess one thing and do another, and, while we are called servants of God, be found by our deeds to be servants of the Devil, he warns and declares that we are servants of the one whose will we fulfill by our works; and it is of no little harm to confess God as Lord, yet in our actions serve the Devil. For God once marks this out and accuses, saying: This people honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me (Isa. 29:13). And the Lord says in the Gospel: No one can serve two masters (Matt. 6:24). And in the Law it is said: God is not mocked (Gal. 6:7).

Romans 6:17-18 But thanks be to God that, though you were slaves of sin, you obeyed from the heart that pattern of teaching into which you were delivered. But by believing in Christ, we have been made servants of righteousness. 

For we are called servants of the one whom we obey. And since it is right to obey Christ (for he himself is righteousness, and the things he commands are righteous), therefore Paul says we have been made servants of righteousness from the heart, not from the law; from the will, not from fear, so that our profession may be brought forth by the judgment of the mind. For we have been led to faith by nature, not by the law, in that pattern of teaching by the command of God by whom we have been made, who fashioned nature. For in nature we have it that we know by whom, through whom, and in whom we have been created. Therefore the pattern of teaching is that into which the Creator has naturally delivered us, that is, what he said above: “They are a law unto themselves” (Rom. 2:14); since they see that what they believe belongs to their nature; so that what the Law and the Prophets preached to the Jews about Christ, this the Gentiles trust in from the heart. Therefore he gives thanks to the Lord, because when we were slaves of sin, we obeyed from the heart by believing in Christ, so that we might serve God, not through the law of Moses, but through the law of nature.

Romans 6:19a I speak in human terms because of the weakness of your flesh. 

When Paul mentions the weakness of the flesh, he means that he demands less of a man than is truly fitting in regard to the worship of God. Finally, it is added:

Romans 6:19b For just as you presented your members to serve impurity and lawlessness, leading to lawlessness, so now present your members to serve righteousness, leading to sanctification. 

In order to take away from us the occasion of fear in approaching the faith, because it might seem to us almost unbearable and harsh, Paul commanded us to serve God by the same measure according to which we formerly served the Devil: although, of course, God ought to be served more zealously than the Devil, since here salvation is at work, there condemnation. Yet the spiritual physician does not demand more from us, lest, as we fled from the commandments as if they were burdensome, considering our weakness, we should remain in death. Therefore the Lord says: Take my yoke upon you . . . for it is sweet, and my burden is light (Matt. 11:29).

Romans 6:20-21 For when you were slaves of sin, you were free in regard to righteousness. For what fruit did you have then from those things of which you are now ashamed? For the end of those things is death. 

It is clear that whoever is free from God is a slave of sin; for while he sins, he departs from God and comes under sin. What then are the fruits of sin, in which, having lived and then learning the good life, we are ashamed, because we once lived shamefully? And not only this, but even the customs of the pagans are base and foul, especially that which was conceived in Phrygia, in which, unless some vile person takes part, the rite is mute and religion grows numb! Behold a freedom full of stains and bound in filth, whose work earns a shameful wage: whose end is death. By “end” he means the conclusion of life and actions, to which either death or life succeeds. But in this place death is double, for one passes from death to death.

Romans 6:22 Now, having been freed from sin but made servants of God, you have your fruit in sanctification, and the end is eternal life.

That is, if, having received the forgiveness of sins, we strive to imitate good deeds, we will attain holiness, and our end, that is, our outcome, will be everlasting life; for from this death, which he called an end, we will pass over into the life that has no end.

Romans 6:23a For the wages of sin is death. 

Paul calls death the profit of sin, because through sin comes death; and therefore those who from now on keep themselves from sins receive as their reward eternal life, because those who do not sin are strangers to the second death.

Romans 6:23b But the grace of God is eternal life in Christ Jesus our Lord. 

For just as those who follow sin obtain death, so those who follow the grace of God, that is, the faith of Christ, which forgives sins, will have eternal life; so that they may rejoice to be dissolved for a time, knowing that they will obtain that life which is free from all weariness and has no succession. Holy Simeon, longing for this, asked to be released from here (Luke 2:29), that he might go in peace, that is, into a life that suffers no disturbance. And our Lord attests that this gift has been given to us by God through Christ, so that we may give thanks to God the Father through no one other than His Son.

Chapter 7

Romans 7:1a You are not ignorant, brothers; I am speaking to those who know the Law. 

To strengthen their minds in divine teaching, Paul uses the example of human law; so that once more through earthly things he may persuade them of heavenly things, just as God is known from the creation of the world. For since the whole belongs to One, although things are diverse, they are nevertheless in some way similar to one another. The Romans therefore know the law, for they are not barbarians: they have grasped natural justice partly from themselves, partly from the Greeks; for the laws were brought to the Romans from Athens, just as to the Greeks from the Hebrews. For before Moses the Law was not hidden, but there was no order nor authority. He therefore speaks not to those who are ignorant of the law:

Romans 7:1b Because the Law rules over a man as long as he lives. 

It is not hidden that the whole life of man is under the law of nature, which was given to the world: this Law is general. Now, however, Paul sets forth another, special law (although it too is general, yet since it is not received by all, it becomes special), through which he wishes to prove his assertion; for he wants to hand on the truth step by step. He therefore says:

Romans 7:2 For the woman who is under a husband is, while her husband lives, bound to the law of her husband. 

This law comes from the Gospel, not from Moses, nor from earthly justice. Therefore, learning some things by the guidance of nature and some things from the law of Moses, they were made perfect through the Gospel of Christ. By the following example, therefore, Paul more clearly urges that Christianity is stripped of the law of works, though not of every law; and that it is no longer advantageous to be under the law, lest by returning to the law one nullify the grace of God; for the grace of God has freed man from the law, so that he may serve God in spirit, that is, in mind. For just as a woman, when her husband is dead, is freed from the law of her husband, though not from the law of nature, so also are those who by the grace of God have been freed from the law by which they were held as guilty; so that it is dead to them, and, being joined to Christianity, they are not adulterers. For if the law is still alive among them, they are adulterers, and it will do them no good to be called Christians, because they will be liable to punishment. For he will not be an adulterer against the law, but against the Gospel, who, after the law has died and he is joined to the Gospel, then returns to the law; for the law is said to be dead when the one whom it held as guilty is pardoned by it, the authority of the law having ceased.

Romans 7:3 But if her husband has died, she is freed from the law of the husband, so that she is not an adulteress if she is joined to another man. 

Once removed from the Law, having received the forgiveness of sins and being joined to the Gospel, they are not adulterers against the Law, because it is dead to them. But if, while keeping the Law, they think they are approaching the Gospel, they will be guilty and adulterers, because with the Law still alive they have joined themselves to faith, and they will be adulterers in both.

Romans 7:4a And so, my brothers, you also have been put to death to the Law through the body of Christ. 

For our Savior allowed His body to be crucified by the Devil, knowing that it was for us and against him; therefore he says that through the body of Christ we have been saved. For to die to the Law is to live to God, because the Law rules over sinners. Whoever, then, has his sins forgiven dies to the Law, that is, is set free from the Law. And so through the body of Christ we obtain this benefit; for when the Savior handed over His body, He conquered death and condemned sin. The Devil sinned in Him, because he killed Him though He was innocent and knew nothing at all of sin; for when he tried to defend man as a cause of sin for himself, he was found guilty in Him whom he accused. From this it came about that all who believe in Christ are delivered from the Law, with sin removed; because sin, which is the Devil, having been defeated through the body of Christ, has no authority over those who belong to Him by whom he was overcome. For when He does not sin and is killed as if guilty, He conquered sin by means of sin, that is, He condemned the Devil by the very sin which he committed in Him; and He erased the handwriting that had been decreed because of Adam’s sin (Col. 2:14), when He rose from the dead, giving to those who believe in Him such a pattern that they cannot be held by the second death. And therefore we have died to the Law through the body of Christ; for whoever has not died to the Law is guilty, and whoever is guilty cannot escape the second death.

Romans 7:4b So that you may belong to another, to Him who rose from the dead, that we might bear fruit for God. 

He says this because we have died to the Law in order that from now on we may be servants of Christ alone; and this will be to bear fruit for God. For one is gained for God who remains in His grace, worthy of the promised resurrection.

Romans 7:5a For when we were in the flesh. 

Since he is in the flesh, that is, in the body, he denies that he is in the flesh; because here “to be in the flesh” is said of one who follows something that the Law forbids. Therefore, to be in the flesh is understood in many ways: for every unbeliever is in the flesh, that is, fleshly; and a Christian living under the Law is in the flesh; and one who places his hope in human beings is in the flesh; and one who misunderstands Christ is in the flesh; and if any Christian leads a life of indulgence, he is in the flesh. Yet in this passage we shall understand “to be in the flesh” in this way: before faith we were in the flesh; for we lived under sin, that is, following fleshly perceptions, we were subject to vices and sins. Now the perception of the flesh is not to believe spiritual things, that is, that a virgin gave birth without the involvement of a man, and that a person is born anew from water and the Holy Spirit, and that a soul released from its bond with the flesh rises again in the flesh. Distrusting these things, one is in the flesh; therefore:

Romans 7:5b The passions, he says, of sins, which are shown through the Law, were at work in our members, so that they might bear fruit unto death.

It is clear that one who does not believe acts under sin, and is dragged captive into spoiling vices, so that he produces fruit for the second death; for death then gains profit when one sins. Yet Paul says that the vices operate in the members, not in the body, lest there be an excuse for those who mistreat the body; for in evil speech the tongue is reproved, in theft the hand is corrected, in deceit the ears are rebuked, and so in other things which, although they proceed from the heart, as the Lord testifies in the Gospel (Matt. 15:18), are nevertheless carried out in deeds by the ministry of the members. This discussion touches the Jews and those who are called Christians and wish to live under the Law; so that, learning that they are fleshly, they may depart from the Law. Yet he shows that the passions of sins, which he says rule those acting in the flesh, are revealed through the Law, not produced by the Law; for the Law is the indicator of sin, not its mother, making sinners guilty.

Romans 7:6a Now, however, we are freed from the Law of death, in which we were held. 

We are freed from the Law when we receive the forgiveness of sins; for it no longer has power over us, since it rules over the faithless and the sinners. The Law of death is therefore so called because it punishes the guilty; it puts sinners to death: it is not evil, then, but just. For although what is inflicted by the Law is an evil for those who suffer it, the Law itself is not evil, because it inflicts wrath justly. Therefore it is not evil for sinners, but just; for the good it is spiritual. For who would doubt that it is something spiritual to forbid sinning? But because it could not do this, that is, it could not save man by remitting sins, the law of faith was given, which would free believers from the dominion of the Law, so that those whom the Law held guilty unto death might be able to be restored to life. For to them the Law is a law of death, since it works wrath on account of sin.

Romans 7:6b So that we may serve in newness of spirit, and not in the oldness of the letter. 

Here Paul is thought to touch on the Law, which indeed he makes inferior by comparison with the law of faith, yet he does not judge it an enemy. For he says that we are rescued from the law of death so that we may serve the law of faith, which has brought us saving protection, something the old law could not do; lest we should seem to make the benefit of grace empty by serving the old law. The old law is certainly not a name of crime, but of time or age; it has grown old because it has ceased. The law of the spirit is itself the law of faith; because faith is in the mind, and is not learned by works, but is believed in the heart: and the mind itself understands that what it believes belongs to its own nature, and it is not seen with the eyes or touched with the hands; and the gifts that are hoped for are not visible nor earthly, but spiritual. The old law, then, was formed on tablets of stone; but the law of the spirit is written spiritually on the tablets of the heart, so that it may be eternal: the letter of the old law is consumed by age. There is also another interpretation of the law of the spirit: namely, that since the former law forbade evil deeds, but this one, when it declares that one must not sin even in the heart, is called the law of the spirit, so that it may make the whole man spiritual.

Romans 7:7a What then shall we say? Is the Law sin? Never. 

Since Paul calls this the law of death, and rejoices that we have been rescued from it, and denies that in it we serve God; lest he should be thought to declare it unjust, he clears this up when he says: Never.

Romans 7:7b But I would not have known sin except through the Law. 

Therefore the Law is not sin, but the indicator of sin; for it shows both the sins that were hidden and that they would not go unpunished before God. Once this was discovered, man became guilty; and for this reason he does not seem to give thanks to the Law. For who gladly welcomes the one who announces that punishment hangs over him? He gives thanks, however, for the law of faith; because the one who was made guilty through the law of Moses has been reconciled to God through the law of faith: although that law too is just and good; for it is good to reveal the danger that threatens. Yet he gives greater thanks to the one through whom, being rescued from danger, he lives.

Romans 7:7c For I did not know desire, except that the Law said in Exodus: You shall not covet (Exod. 20:17).

Paul did not distinguish this desire from sin, but blended them, signifying this: that since there was not even the suspicion that such a thing was not permitted before God, I learned, he says, that it is sin. Under his own person he pleads, as it were, a general case. Therefore the Law forbids desire which, because it is a delight, was not thought to be sin. For the matter seemed simple: to desire something belonging to one’s neighbor; but the Law revealed that this is sin. For to worldly men nothing seems so unavenged and so pleasant as pleasure.

Romans 7:8a Therefore, when the opportunity was taken, sin, through the commandment, produced in me every desire. 

By saying “every desire,” Paul signifies all sins. For above he mentions desire according to the Law, and now, by adding the other vices, he says that every desire was produced in man by the impulse of the Devil, whom he signifies in the word “sin”; so that the Law, which was given to be beneficial, turned into the opposite for man. For when the Devil saw that help had been provided for man through the Law, man whom he rejoiced to have in his condition both on account of Adam’s sin and on account of the man’s own, he understood that what had been done was against him. When he saw that man had been placed under the Law, he knew for sure that his own dominion had been taken away; for man had recognized how he might escape the punishment of hell [inferni]. From this his anger blazed up against man, so that he might overturn the Law for him; that, by committing what was forbidden, he might again offend God and once more fall into the power of the Devil. He began not to command, but to deceive subtly; for when the Law was given, the Devil lost his dominion, knowing that from then on man belonged to God’s judgment.

Romans 7:8b For without the Law sin was dead. 

This must be understood in a twofold way, so that you may know that the Devil is signified in “sin,” and also sin itself, which is called sin. For the Devil is said to have been dead, because he ceased from deceiving man before the Law, but was as if quiet; for he was secure about his possession of him. But he said that sin was dead, because it was thought that it was not credited before God; therefore it was dead in the eyes of man, as though one sinned with freedom. For sin was not hiding, as I said above; but it was unknown in this respect, that God was going to be the Judge. When the Law was given, it became clear that sin had come back to life. Why did it come back to life, if not because it had lived before, and afterwards, through the neglect of men, was considered dead when it was alive? For it was thought that sin was not credited, when in fact it was credited. Therefore, what was living was regarded as dead.

Romans 7:9a But I myself once lived without the Law. 

What does “without the Law” mean, since the Law has always existed, as I mentioned above, except that man lived without the fear of God, secure in the belief that God would not judge human actions.

Romans 7:9b But when the commandment came, sin came back to life. 

Paul therefore said that sin “came back to life” to show that it had previously lived; afterward, however, through human negligence it was considered dead, though it was alive, just as false rumor commonly arises about those who are far away. This means that in the beginning sin was not hidden from being reckoned before God, that is, it lived; but when the habit of sinning had blurred this, it was thought dead, so that the crime was believed to cease together with the person. But when the Law was given or renewed, it came back to life among those by whom it had been considered dead; for they began to know that sin was being reckoned.

Romans 7:10a But I myself have died. 

Man is dead, because he sees that he is guilty before God, when previously he thought that he would not be liable for the things he was sinning in.

Romans 7:10b And I discovered that the commandment, which had been given for life, is in fact unto death. 

It is true that the Law was given for life; but when it made man guilty of sin not only in regard to the past but also to the future, the Law that was given for life came to be unto death, but, as I said, for the one who sins; for those who obey, it leads to life.

Romans 7:11 For sin, having taken the opportunity through the commandment, deceived me, and through it killed me. 

In this place understand “sin” as the Devil, who is the author of sin. He found an opportunity through the law to satisfy his cruelty by the killing of man: so that, because the Law threatens sinners, man, always committing what is forbidden under his impulse, by offending God would experience the Law’s vengeance; and so would be condemned by that which had been given to benefit him. For since the Law was given against his will, envy flared up in him against man, so that he might stain him even more with corrupt pleasures, lest he escape his grasp.

Romans 7:12 And so the Law indeed is holy, and the commandment holy, and just, and good. 

In order that no hostile suspicion should remain against the Law, Paul praises it in such a way that he declares it not only just, but also holy and good. For the Gospel discourse bears witness that by “commandment” the Law is to be understood. For it says: If you wish to enter into life, keep the commandments (Matt. 19:17).

Romans 7:13a Therefore, is that which is good death to me? Far from it. 

Truly far from it; for how could that which is proven to be good be understood as death?

Romans 7:13b But in order that sin might appear as sin, it worked death in me through what is good. 

Paul is saying that the Devil, through what is good, took the opportunity to work evil against man, as he seduced him to death; because the Law was given and revealed in written letters so that the things done badly before the Law might be shown, through the enticement of desire, to be sins, and thereafter be avoided. Then Satan was inflamed, indeed through what is good; for he saw that requirement was being made for man, and he urged him toward unlawful things, so that from the Law, which was given for life, death might result for him. This, however, must be credited to human negligence, which so weakened the vigor of its nature through the desire to sin that it could not restrain the suggestions of the adversary. Yet the enemy, whom he implies in sin so that he may be seen to be an enemy, took from the Law the opportunity to work death for man. For as he urged him toward unlawful things, he appears as an enemy. Although even before the Law he had acquired death for man, apart from the first cause in Adam, yet after the Law he created heavier punishments for him in the underworld [inferos], where the second death is. For it is a lesser offense to have sinned before the Law was revealed than after the Law.

Romans 7:13c So that sin might become, beyond measure, sinning through the commandment (Gen. 15:16).

What does “beyond measure” mean, as if some measure were given to wrongdoers, when it is never permitted to sin? But these are God’s words, saying: “The sins of the Amorites are not yet complete” (Gen. 15:16). By this he shows that there is a certain measure of offenses, and when sinners have filled it up, they are judged utterly unworthy of life; just as in the case of Pharaoh, because he had filled up this measure, God’s powers and signs were shown in him, by which others, terrified, might apply themselves to what leads to life, that life might be learned from the one who died. But there is another measure that the Apostle is discussing; for in order to show that sin under the Law is more than it was before the Law, he indicates that beyond this measure of sins, a greater measure has increased after the Law through the envy and cunning of Satan; so that the providence of God might be turned against man, when under the fear of the Law he ought by no means to have sinned. Therefore God, so that he might overcome the envy of the Devil and confirm his providence toward man, changed the order, sending the Savior Christ, who would both conquer the Devil and establish God’s decrees concerning man.

Romans 7:14a For we know that the Law is spiritual. 

Since Paul speaks to those who know the Law, he therefore says: We know that the Law is spiritual; for they would not place themselves under the Law unless they recognized that it is spiritual. He calls the Law of Moses spiritual, the Law that was given on tablets: because it forbids sin, it is spiritual, since it forbids the worship of visible and carnal things. These remarks serve to praise the Law, lest someone perhaps think it worthy of blame, because it exercised severity against sinners.

Romans 7:14b But I am carnal, sold under sin. 

Paul calls a man carnal when he sins. Sold under sin. This is to be sold under sin, to draw one’s origin from Adam, who first sinned, and to become subject to sin by one’s own fault, as the prophet Isaiah says: You have been sold, he says, by your sins (Isa. 50:1). For Adam first sold himself, and therefore all his seed is subject to sin. Therefore man is weak for keeping the precepts of the Law unless he is fortified by divine help; from this it is that he says: The Law is spiritual; but I am carnal, sold under sin. That is, the Law is firm and just and free from blame, but man is frail and is enslaved by his father’s or his own fault, so that he cannot exercise control over himself with regard to obedience to the Law. Therefore we must flee to the mercy of God, that we may escape the severity of the Law, and, being relieved of our sins, may thereafter, with God’s favor, resist the enemy. For what does it mean to be subject to sin, if not to have a body corrupted by the fault of the soul, into which sin inserts itself and drives man, as though captive, into sins, so that he does its will? Therefore the Lord, among other things, says: The Devil comes and takes away what has been sown in their heart, so that they may not be saved (Luke 8:12). From this the same Apostle says in another letter: Our struggle is not against flesh and blood, but against principalities and powers, against the spiritual forces of wickedness in the heavenly places (Eph. 6:12), who are known to be the minions of Satan. For before man’s transgression, before he enslaved himself to death, these had no power to enter into the inner man and insert hostile thoughts. That is why, by his cunning, it came about that he deceived man by conversing through the serpent (Gen. 3:1). But after he had deceived and subdued him, he received power over him, so as to strike the inner man, joining himself to his mind, so that man cannot recognize what in his thoughts is his own and what is the Devil’s, unless he looks to the Law.

Romans 7:15a For what I do, I do not understand. 

He does not understand what he does, because he sees that he knows one thing through the Law and does another.

Romans 7:15b For I do not do what I will, but what I hate, that I do. 

Being subject to sin, he indeed does what he does not will.

Romans 7:16 But if I do what I hate, I consent to the Law. 

Paul proves that the Law rightly forbids, when he confesses that he unwillingly does what the Law prohibits; and he says that what the Law commands belongs to his nature, because he declares that what he does outwardly is hateful to himself.

Romans 7:17 But now it is no longer I who do it, but the sin that dwells in me. 

For by the law Paul sees that what he does is forbidden, and he agrees that it ought not to be done, and therefore shows that there is something else by whose impulse these things are done, that is, sin. But he always calls it sin, although it is known to be the Devil with his angels; because if the first man had not sinned, these things would not have come about, and therefore it is sin that brings all these things to pass.

Romans 7:18a For I know that no good dwells in me, that is, in my flesh. 

Paul does not say, as some imagine, that the flesh itself is evil; but that what dwells in the flesh is not good, but sin. How does sin dwell in the flesh, since it is not a substance, but a departure from the good? Because the body of the first man was corrupted through sin so that it could be dissolved: the very corruption of sin, by the condition of the offense, remains in the body, holding the strength of the divine sentence given to Adam, which is the mark of the Devil, at whose instigation he sinned. Therefore, through that which remains as the consequence of what was done, sin is said to dwell in the flesh, to which the Devil draws near as to his own law, and remains as sin within sin; because the flesh already belongs to sin, so that it may deceive man with evil suggestions, lest man do what the Law commands.

Romans 7:18b For the will to do good is present in me. 

Paul declares that the things which the Law commands are so good that he naturally finds them pleasing and wants to do them.

Romans 7:18c But I do not find the power to accomplish the good. 

So what is commanded by the Law pleases him, and there is the will to carry it out; yet to fulfill it, the strength and power are lacking, because he is so pressed down by the power of sin that he cannot go where he wishes, nor is he able to resist, since another is lord over his power. For man is now burdened by the habit of sinning, and more easily yields to sin than to the Law, which he knows teaches what is good; and if he does wish to do good, habit presses him down, with the enemy as his helper.

Romans 7:19 For I do not do the good that I want, but the evil that I do not want, this I do. 

Paul often repeats this, so that he may make it clear. This, then, is what was said above: that as a captive, through the above-mentioned causes, he is forced to do that which sin desires.

Romans 7:20 But if I do what I do not want, it is no longer I who do it, but the sin that dwells in me. 

This is what he already mentioned above: because, being pressed down and subdued by sin, he carries out not his own will but its will. Now, because he says that a man sins unwillingly, should he appear free from guilt, since he does what he does not want, being pressed by the force of its power? Certainly not; for these things began through his own fault and laziness. For since he has enslaved himself to sin by giving his consent, it rightly rules over him; first it persuades, so that when he is overcome it may then rule. But the Apostle, in order to set the grace of God before us, explains these things to show from how great evils man has been delivered, and what destructions he draws from Adam, and what benefits he has obtained through Christ; so that it might be shown that not even the Law was able to bring him help.

Romans 7:21 I find then the law, that when I want to do good, evil is present with me. 

Paul declares that he gives the Law of Moses the consent of his will against the sin that dwells in the flesh, which compels him to do something other than what man and the Law desire.

Romans 7:22 For I take delight in the Law of God according to the inner man. 

Paul says that the mind is pleased with those things that are handed down by the Law: this is the inner man; because sin does not dwell in the mind, but in the flesh, which comes from the origin of the flesh of sin, and through transmission all flesh becomes flesh of sin. For if the soul itself were from transmission, and sin dwelt in it, since the soul of Adam sinned more than the body, then the sin of the soul would have corrupted the body. Therefore sin dwells in the flesh, as it were at the doors of the soul, so that it does not allow it to go where it wills. But if it dwelt in the soul, man would never come to know himself; now, however, he knows himself and takes delight in the Law of God.

Romans 7:23 But I see another law in my members, warring against the law of my mind and leading me captive to the law of sin that is in my members. 

Paul mentions two laws, of which he insists he sees one in his members, that is, in the outer man, who is flesh or body. This is the law that is opposed, that resists the soul, dragging it captive into the condition of sin, so that, going no further, it may not find a defender. The other is the law of the mind, which is the law of Moses or the natural law, which is in the soul. This is pressed down by the violence of sin, indeed by its own negligence, because while it loves vices it subjects itself to sin, so that by that very habit it is held captive; for habit rules over a person. He names four laws: first, the spiritual law, which is also natural, which was renewed through Moses and placed in authority; this is the Law of God. Next, the law of the mind that agrees with the Law of God. Third, the law of sin, which he says dwells in the members as a result of the transgression of the first man. And fourth, that which is seen in the members when it suggests evil and then withdraws. But these laws, repeated, seem to be four, though they are two, that is, good and evil. For the law of the mind is itself the spiritual law, or that of Moses, which is called the Law of God; while the law of sin is the same as that other one which he says is seen in the members, which contradicts the law of our mind.

Romans 7:24-25a Unhappy man that I am, who will deliver me from the body of this death? The grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord. 

Paul calls the man unhappy because he is born under sin. Is not man truly unhappy, who has come into this inheritance of transgression, having sin as an enemy with him, through which Satan has access to him? For Adam constructed steps by which the despoiler ascends to his sons, unless the most merciful Lord, moved by mercy, had given His grace through Christ; so that the human race, lifted up by the reception of the forgiveness of sins, might from then on be able to recover its senses, with sin repressed and condemned. For once lightened of evils and purified, he can resist the adversary, having received power against him, while he is helped by God. Here he introduces, as it were, a third law of faith, more powerful, which he also calls grace: which nevertheless has its origin from the spiritual law; because through it man is set free, so that since Moses gave the law, and the Lord also gave the law, they may be called two; yet one is understood, as far as meaning and providence are concerned. For that is the initiator of salvation, but this is the perfecter. But I do not mean that part of the law which is in new moons and in circumcision and in foods, but that which pertains to the mystery of God and to discipline. Therefore by the grace of God through Christ man is freed from the body of this death. He calls that death the one that he showed above as found in the killing of man through sin in the underworld [inferos], which is called the second death: but the body of death is all sins; for many are one body, the individual sins as it were members, found under one author, from which man, snatched away by the grace of God through baptism, has escaped the aforesaid death. For since he is placed in a fleshly body, he would not say that he has been snatched away from that very body: but he called this the body which above he signifies is destroyed through baptism and the observance of the Law. For when he says: From the body of death, he shows that there is another body that is not of death.

Romans 7:25b Therefore I myself with my mind serve the law of God.

When Paul says the law of God, he means both the law of Moses and that of Christ. But with the flesh the law of sin. I myself, that is, I who am freed from the body of death. He is in the body; how has he been freed from the body of death, if not because he has been freed from all evils? For the forgiveness of sins removes all sins. Therefore, freed from the body of death by the grace of God through Christ, with the mind or soul I serve the law of God, but with the flesh the law of sin, that is, of the Devil, who through the flesh subjected to him inserts evil suggestions into the soul. Since therefore man is twofold, consisting of flesh and mind, he says that he serves the law of God with his mind; because the mind is devoted to God, and having recovered its own power, it can oppose the sin which works through the flesh. For the flesh is corrupted and subject to death, which had been made in such a way that, by the association and union with the soul, it would not die; it has received desires, the burden of which it as it were transfers to the mind, so that it too is weighed down. But what rejects that burden? The death of the grace of God.

Romans 7:25c With my mind I serve the law of God, but with my flesh the law of sin, 

As has been said. For now the soul is free, and recalled to good habit, and with the Holy Spirit helping, it can scorn evil suggestions; for authority has been given back to it, so that it may dare to resist the enemy. For he who is no longer liable cannot at all be tracked down against his will. But the flesh, since it has no judgment, nor is capable of discernment (for it is a harsh nature), cannot close the way to the enemy; lest when he comes he enter, and suggest contrary things to the mind, and through this: With the flesh, he says, I serve the law of sin. For since one man consists of flesh and soul, from that part by which he is wise, he serves God; but from the other part by which he is foolish, he serves the law of sin. But if man had remained in that condition in which he was made, there would be no power for the enemy to approach his flesh and whisper contrary things to his soul. But so that the whole man should not be restored by the grace of Christ to his former state, the sentence given against Adam stood in the way (Gen. 3:17); for it would have been unjust to annul a sentence lawfully pronounced. Therefore, with the sentence remaining, by the providence of God a remedy was found, so that salvation might be given back to man, which he had lost by his own fault; so that, healed here, he might believe that his adversary, defeated by the power of Christ, would not dare, once the sentence of the first death has been pierced, to lay claim to man for himself, when the race of Adam has been united, lest man be restored to the condition of his first origin, now remaining wholly immortal.

Chapter 8

Romans 8:1 Therefore there is now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus, who do not walk according to the flesh. 

It is true, because there is no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus, diligently serving the law of God.

Romans 8:2 For the law of the Spirit of life in Christ Jesus has set me free from the law of sin and of death. 

Grace from God demands security for a man, so that he may not be anxious about the suggestions of the Devil, provided that he despises them; for they will do a man no harm at the second death; because the law of faith, that is, the Spirit, has freed man from the second death, once sin has been condemned: nor does it now harm a man that sin is in the flesh, if only he resists it, mindful of the help of God: indeed, he is also to be crowned who presses down the schemes of sin remaining in the flesh; for it is great skill to avoid the snares of a household enemy. Therefore the law of the Spirit of life is itself the law of faith: for the law of Moses is also spiritual, because it forbids sinning; yet it is not of life, because it cannot remit sins for those guilty of death, so as to make those destined to die live. This one, however, because it both wills that one not sin, and calls back from death, is called the law of the Spirit of life, consisting not in letter but in spirit; because it is believed in the heart, and what is believed is spirit. This Law therefore in Christ Jesus, that is, through faith in Christ, frees the believer from the law of sin and of death. The law of sin is that which he says dwells in the members, which strives to persuade what is contrary: but the law of death is the law of Moses, because it puts sinners to death. For even in another epistle among other things he speaks of the authority of the law: If the ministry of death, carved in stones, was in glory (2 Cor. 3:7).

It is therefore not a matter for wonder if it is called a spiritual law and a law of death, since the Gospel is likewise so. For in another place among other things he says: To some an odor of life unto life, to others an odor of death unto death (2 Cor. 2:16). For those whom the reputation of virtues, or the sight or the hearing of deeds, attracted to faith, for them the Gospel was an odor of life; but for those whose mind the accomplished power inflamed to contradict, the preaching of faith was an odor of death. Therefore, though faith is one, it produces diversity among men; just as the sun, though it is one, melts wax but hardens clay. In whatever disposition, then, one has perceived faith as an odor, so too will it benefit him. But it is perhaps said: If faith bestows the same thing as the Law, why is it not also itself in some respect called a law of death; for does it not put to death non-believers? But it is not so. For faith is given which justifies those who take refuge in it; that it may remit for them those whom the Law holds guilty; so that those who act under faith may be free from sin: for those who act under the Law are liable. Those, therefore, who do not obey faith, are not put to death by faith, but by the Law; because, not approaching faith, he is held guilty by the Law: and so all who with an evil mind perceive the words of faith as an odor remain in death.

Let us now see what difference there is when it is called a spiritual law and a law of the Spirit. This is the difference, that it is called a spiritual law for this reason: because it gives precepts through which one does not sin, since he who does not sin is called spiritual; a rival of things above, that is, of heavenly things. But it is called the law of the Spirit because God, whose faith it is, is Spirit. There, therefore, are words; here, the reality: there, the things that are God’s; here, God himself.

Romans 8:3a Since what was impossible for the Law, in which it was weakened through the flesh, God, sending His Son in the likeness of sinful flesh, condemned sin in the flesh on account of sin. 

Paul says this to make the baptized confident, because they have been freed from sin. Since what was impossible, he says, for the Law. For whom was it impossible? For us, of course, it was impossible to fulfill the commandment of the Law, because we were subject to sin. For this reason God sent His Son in the likeness of sinful flesh. This is the likeness of flesh: because although it is the same flesh as ours, yet it was not formed in the womb and born as our flesh is. For it was sanctified in the womb and born without sin, and He Himself did not sin in it. For this reason a virginal womb was chosen for the birth of the Lord, so that in holiness the flesh of the Lord might differ from our flesh; for it is similar in its cause, not in the quality of the substance of sin. Therefore he called it likeness, because though of the same substance of flesh, it did not have the same nativity; for the body of the Lord was not subject to sin. For the flesh of the Lord was purified by the Holy Spirit, so that He might be born in such a body as Adam had before sin; although the sentence alone was passed on Adam. Therefore, when God sent Christ,

Romans 8:3b He condemned sin because of sin;

That is, He condemned sin by its own sin. For when Christ is crucified by sin, which is Satan, sin committed its sin in the flesh of the Savior’s body: by this act God condemned sin in the flesh, precisely where it sinned, just as he says in another epistle: Triumphing over them in him (Col. 2:15); that is, in Christ. For it is customary to say of anyone condemned: In what matter was he condemned? The answer is given, for example, in murder. So also sin was condemned in the flesh, that is, in the sin which it committed in the flesh. By this sin, therefore, Satan was made guilty and lost his dominion over the souls he held, so that he no longer dares to hold, in the second death, those already sealed with the sign of the cross, by which he was defeated. For this reason the Apostle discusses these things for our assurance.

Romans 8:4 That the justification of the Law might be fulfilled in us, who do not walk according to the flesh, but according to the spirit. 

Therefore Paul says that sin is condemned, so that in us might be fulfilled the justification of the law given by Moses; for, being lifted out of the condition of the Law, we have been made its friends, for the friends of the Law are justified. But how is justification fulfilled in us, unless when the forgiveness of all sins is given; so that, sins being removed, the person may appear justified, serving the law of God in mind? This is not to walk according to the flesh, but according to the spirit; so that the devotion of the soul, which is spirit, does not consent to the desire of sin; because through the flesh are sown the desires of the soul, in which sin is present. For if sin is condemned by the Savior, how is it present? It is condemned by the Savior, and condemned in a threefold manner. First, he condemned sin in this respect, that he did not sin, dissenting from sin. Then upon the cross, sin is said to have been condemned because it sinned. Where its authority as it were of sin, by which it held men in hell [inferno] on account of Adam’s offense, has been removed from it; so that from now on, in those in whom the sign of the cross is found, it may not dare to hold them. Thirdly, he condemned sin, in that he made offenses null and void by granting the remission of sins. For since the sinner, by reason of the sin committed, ought to be condemned, by forgiving him he condemned the sin in him. And so, if we too, following the example of the Savior, do not sin, we condemn sin.

Romans 8:5a For those who are according to the flesh, they think on the things of the flesh. 

Paul says this because whoever obeys the suggestion that arises through the flesh, thinks on the things of the flesh. For that which follows, desiring use against what is right and the judgment of the error of the flesh, seems sweet to him; for in the flesh worldly error is marked in every respect.

Romans 8:5b But those who are according to the spirit, feel the things that are of the spirit. 

It is clear that whoever restrains himself from the desires of the flesh lives according to the law of the mind; for it seems useful to him to be wise in what agrees with the Law.

Romans 8:6a For the wisdom of the flesh is death. 

The wisdom of the flesh is sin, which brings forth death. For it is called wisdom, though it is a foolish thing, because to worldly men the errors conceived from visible things, whether in thought or in deed against the law of God, seem to be wisdom: especially because all their effort and cunning is directed toward sinning. For they think themselves to be wise if they pursue this more diligently, though nothing is more foolish than to sin. There is also another wisdom of the flesh, which, puffed up by worldly reasoning, denies that something can be done, so that the world may lack reason for it. Therefore it mocks the Virgin birth, the resurrection of the flesh.

Romans 8:6b But the wisdom of the Spirit is life and peace. 

Truly this is wisdom, which gains life and peace; for by following spiritual things, with the enticements of the present life despised, one will have eternal life with peace, that is, without disturbance; where there is no turmoil and no punishment.

Romans 8:7 Since the wisdom of the flesh is hostile to God; for it is not subject to the law of God, nor indeed can it be. 

Paul did not say the flesh is hostile, but the wisdom of the flesh; that is, not the substance, but either evil actions, or thought, or assertion that is born of error. Therefore, the wisdom of the flesh is, in the first place, the speculation about the stars discovered by men, and then the delight in visible things. These are hostile to God, because they make the Lord of the elements and the maker of the world equal to the things He has made, asserting that nothing can be done beyond what the order of the world contains. Therefore they deny that God made it so that a Virgin gave birth, or that the bodies of the dead should rise again; because, they say, it is foolish that God should do anything beyond what man understands. O you wise of the world, who think that God ought not to act otherwise than does the creature made by Him, so that He Himself may be thought similar to the creatures! Therefore they are blinded, so that they do not see what an insult they offer to God. For the work which He agreed to do for the proclamation of His own praise, these men, disapproving it, declare to be unbelievable and foolish: and therefore this wisdom of the flesh cannot be subject to the law of God; for they sharpen their zeal to this end, that they may oppose the deeds of God.

Romans 8:8 For those who are in the flesh cannot please God. 

The wise of the world are in the flesh, because they devote themselves to the wisdom of the world, through which they resist the law of God. For whatever is against the law of God is carnal; because it is from the world; for the whole world is flesh: indeed, everything visible is assigned to the flesh; for they are kindred to the flesh, or rather elements of the flesh. And therefore whoever obeys worldly things is in the flesh.

Romans 8:9a But you are not in the flesh, but in the Spirit.

 Though placed in the flesh, they are said not to be in the flesh if, agreeing with the apostle John, they do not love worldly things (1 Jn. 2:15); for a person’s judgment, as it were, shapes his own nature, so that he is called what he thinks.

Romans 8:9b If indeed the Spirit of God dwells in you. 

Paul says this somewhat obscurely because, having been introduced to the Law, their faith was not yet perfect; but he saw in them the hope of perfection. Therefore sometimes he speaks as to those already perfect, sometimes as to those about to be perfected, that is, sometimes he praises, sometimes he admonishes; so that if, according to what was mentioned above, they conduct themselves according to the law of nature, they may be said to be in the Spirit; because the Spirit of God cannot dwell in one who follows carnal things.

Romans 8:9c But if anyone does not have the Spirit of Christ, this one is not his.

The Spirit whom above Paul called the Spirit of God, he now calls the Spirit of Christ, because all things of the Father are the Son’s. This, then, is what he says: that whoever is subject to the above-mentioned errors does not belong to Christ; for he does not have His Spirit, which he had received that he might be a son of God. For the Holy Spirit abandons a person for two reasons: when he has either the mind of the flesh or the deeds of the flesh. And so by these admonitions he urges them to good conduct; for although men are raised up by the goodness of God, yet the sons of God who are called are burdened, unless they so live that they may not appear unworthy of the name granted to them.

Romans 8:10 The body indeed is dead because of sin, but the Spirit is true life because of justification. 

On account of sin he declares the body dead for those whom the Holy Spirit abandons, nor does he allow the suffering of their death to reach that One, that is, the Spirit. For the Spirit of God does not know how to sin; it is given for justification, so that by its help it may justify. Therefore, because it does not know how to sin, it is life; it cannot die, because through sin comes death, and therefore the sinner harms himself, not the Spirit which he has received. For the Spirit will not be the cause, since he wills to justify. This is the sign of justification in a man, that through that which dwells in him he may appear, being justified, to be a son of God; for in a insincere man, nor in a body subject to sins, the Holy Spirit cannot dwell, as Solomon says (Wisdom 1:4). But if anyone again lives carnally, abandoned by the Holy Spirit, he will die in his unrighteousness. Now by saying “body,” he signifies the whole man as dead because of sin, just as by the prophet under the name of “soul” the whole man is signified, as if the whole were expressed by a part; for he says: The soul that sins, it shall die (Ezek. 18:4). Is it without the body? Here he has put “body” for the whole man, there “soul.” And another prophet, by saying “flesh,” has signified the whole man; for he says: All flesh shall see the salvation of God (Isa. 40:5). And since the Holy Spirit is given to the baptized, it is said that the spirit is also his. Therefore he called the other parts of man “body” in comparison with it, because the soul, when it sins, is called “flesh”; for it is called by that which it follows, as I have often already said. In a similar manner also the Lord, when in his passion he did not wish his divinity to be understood as fearing, but that the sadness was of the man, said: The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh weak (Matt. 26:41); so that in “spirit” he might signify God, and in “flesh” man.

Romans 8:11 But if the Spirit of him who raised Jesus from the dead dwells in you, he who raised Jesus from the dead will also give life to your mortal bodies because of his Spirit dwelling in you. 

Paul treats the same meaning in the same way; for he said “bodies,” by which he signifies human beings. And because above he said that on account of sin the body dies the second death, here again he has promised that on account of the good life the mortal bodies will be given life, that is, the whole human being.

Romans 8:12 Therefore, brothers, we are debtors not to the flesh, to live according to the flesh. 

It is right and clear that we ought not to follow the example of Adam, who acted carnally and, by sinning first, left death to us as an inheritance; rather, we ought to serve the law of Christ, who by spiritual means redeemed us from the death mentioned above. For we are debtors to him, who justified us, stained with carnal vices, by washing us clean through the Spirit, and made us children of God. For formerly, placed in the flesh, we lived subject to sins, following Adam’s example; but now, having been freed, we must render obedience to the Redeemer. This obedience certainly benefits not him, who needs nothing, but obtains eternal life for us; for he loves us so much that he attributes to himself what benefits us.

Romans 8:13a For if you live according to the flesh, you will die. 

Nothing is truer, because if we live according to Adam, we will die; for Adam, transgressing, sold himself to sin, assigned to the flesh. For every sin is flesh; therefore, because vices and transgressions arise outwardly from the senses, that is, from hearing, sight, touch, smell, or taste, they are assigned to the flesh. For every thought looking outward suffers transgression; indeed, the sin of the first man was also born outwardly. To live according to the flesh, therefore, is death; for every act of the flesh is outside the Law.

Romans 8:13b But if by the Spirit you put to death the deeds of the flesh, you will live. 

The body wishes to be governed by the law of the soul; therefore he shows that if, under the guidance of the Holy Spirit, the deeds and counsels of the flesh, which are devised at the instigation of the powers of this world, are restrained so that they do not receive the power to act, life is granted. They are said to be put to death if they cease: for they do not exist if they cease; because there is no sin if it is not committed.

Romans 8:14 For all who are led by the Spirit of God are sons of God. 

Paul says that those are led by the Spirit of God in whose actions the designs of the rulers and powers of this world are not seen; for in those in whom they are seen, they are not sons of God, but of the Devil, because “whoever is born of God does not sin,” says the apostle John (1 Jn. 3:9). For this is what proves the sons of God and of the Devil. Therefore the Lord said to the Jews, who were doing evil and plotting murder: “You are of your father the Devil” (Jn. 8:44).

Romans 8:15a For you did not receive a spirit of slavery again in fear. 

Paul says this because, having received the Holy Spirit, we have been freed from fear of evil deeds, so that from now on we do nothing of that kind for which we might again be afraid. Formerly we were under fear, because, once the Law was given, all were made guilty. Therefore he called the Law the spirit of fear, because it placed people in fear on account of sin. But the law of faith, signified by the spirit of adoption, is the law of security, because it frees us from fear by forgiving sins and thereby makes us secure; and for this reason it is called the spirit not of fear.

Romans 8:15b But you received the Spirit of adoption as sons, in whom we cry out: Abba, Father. 

Freed from fear by God’s grace, we have received the Spirit of adoption as sons, so that, considering what we were and what we have attained by God’s gift, we may order our lives with great diligence, lest the name of God the Father suffer insult in us, and lest, like the ungrateful, we fall again into all those things from which we escaped. For we have obtained such grace that we dare say to God: Abba, that is, Father! Therefore he warns us not to turn the confidence we have received into assumption; for if we display a life unlike this utterance by which we say, “Abba, Father,” we insult God by calling him Father. For this is why, in his goodness, he granted us what is beyond our nature: so that, though unworthy by our substance, we may become worthy by our deeds.

Romans 8:16 The Spirit Himself bears witness to our spirit that we are children of God.
