When we act rightly, and the Holy Spirit Himself remains in us, He bears witness to this voice and to our spirit, with which we cry out in prayer: “Abba,” that is, “Father,” while He remains in us, because we do not say “Abba, Father” rashly. Let us indeed present a life worthy of this cry; and this is the testimony of children, if through the Spirit the fatherly mark is seen in them.

Romans 8:17a But if children, then heirs: heirs indeed of God, and fellow heirs with Christ. 

Since God the Father can in no way be called dead, while Christ, his Son, is called dead because of the Incarnation, how is it that he who died is said to be the heir of him who always lives, since heirs are surely heirs only of the dead? But this concerns humanity, not divinity. For with God what is called inheritance among us is a gift of the Father transferred to obedient sons, so that the living may be heir of the living by his own merit, not by the necessity of the deceased. Therefore the Lord in the Gospel, by no less a human than divine meaning, indicated in a parable that he had divided his substance among the living (Luke 15:12); for a parable is not composed with an absurd meaning. Therefore, to make us ready to obey God the Father, he exhorts us with this hope, saying that we shall be heirs of God and fellow heirs with Christ; so that, because the hope of the reward is great, we might be all the more inclined toward the things of God, putting worldly concerns second. What it means to be a fellow heir of the Son of God is taught us by the apostle John; among other things he says: We know that when he appears, we shall be like him (I Jn. 3:2).

Romans 8:17b If, however, we suffer with him, so that we may also be glorified together. 

Paul explains how we can become fellow heirs with Christ, saying: If, however, we suffer with him, so that we may also be glorified together. Let us therefore see what it means to suffer with him. To suffer with him is to endure persecutions for the hope of future things, and to crucify the flesh with its vices and desires, that is, to despise the pleasures and displays of the world. For when all these things have died in a person, he crucifies the world, believing in the life of the age to come, in which he hopes to become a fellow heir with Christ.

Romans 8:18 I therefore consider that the sufferings of this present time are not worthy of the future glory, which will be revealed in us. 

This encouragement refer to the previous, by which he shows that the things which can be inflicted here by the faithless are small in comparison with the reward decreed in the age to come. Therefore Paul commands us to be prepared for every calamity, because great rewards have been promised for these things; so that in tribulation the spirit may console itself and grow through hope. For we know that some, indeed many, for present gains, which will certainly profit only briefly, undertake harsh and difficult labors, yet sometimes cannot reach what they strive for, since life itself is fragile and uncertain. For what advantages do sailors entrust themselves to such storms and tempests, when they know that in them death is more before their eyes than life? And soldiers, in hope of a present reward, although victory is uncertain, do not hesitate to rush upon swords. How much more, then, ought one to suffer for Christ, whose benefits have also been delayed for us: who promises magnificent things in place of small ones, heavenly things in place of earthly ones, and eternal rewards with glory in place of temporal ones! We are said to suffer for Christ when what we suffer does not benefit him; rather, because he seeks an opportunity to reward us, he commands this. For, being a good and generous giver, he seeks a reason by which he may give even to the unworthy or the timid. Finally, so that the calamities themselves may be endured, he himself supplies the strength.

Romans 8:19 For the creation awaits the revelation of the sons of God. 

For Paul said that the sufferings of the present time are unworthy of the glory that is to come; he added that the creation awaits this, so that the number of the sons of God destined for life may be completed; then at last creation itself too may be freed from slavery to corruption and cease from its defect.

Romans 8:20 For creation was subjected to vanity, not willingly. 

Creation, placed under the power of the Lord and Creator, was certainly not subjected to vanity of its own accord; for what has been subjected will not benefit itself, but us. What, then, is the vanity to which it was subjected, unless it is that the things it produces are perishable? For it works to produce corruptible fruits: corruption itself, therefore, is vanity; for all things that are born in the world are weak, perishable, and corruptible, and therefore vain. For they are vain because they cannot retain their own state: indeed, all things, disfigured by continual change, return confused into nature. Solomon also says this about these things, that all these are vanity (Eccles. 1:2). David does not disagree with him, saying: “Nevertheless every living man is vanity” (Ps. 38:6). For is it not vanity to eat and drink, and to care for the affairs of the world? Yet vanity itself is beneficial; for it helps those born in the world so that, exercised in their bodies through these things, they may learn the mystery of the Creator. For in comparison with eternal things they are vain; but in themselves they are good, since they are necessary.

Romans 8:21a But because of him who subjected it in hope. 

Paul immediately explained what this hope is, saying:

Romans 8:21b For creation itself will also be set free from the slavery of corruption into the freedom of the glory of the children of God.

 For creation cannot contradict its Creator; it has been subjected for his sake, not without hope. For, having been placed in labor, it has this comfort: that it will have rest when all whom God knows will believe have believed; for whose sake also he subjected it.

Romans 8:22 For we know that every creature groans and is in labor until now. 

To be in labor is to suffer. This is the meaning, because every creature groans and suffers through daily labor until the present time. “Until now” signifies for as long as it is read. For even the elements perform their tasks with anxiety; the sun and moon fulfill the spaces appointed to them not without labor, and the spirit of living creatures is constrained with a great groan to perform its services; for we see those groaning things compelled unwillingly to work. All these, therefore, await rest, that they may be freed from servile labor. But if this servitude were one that advanced toward pleasing God, creation would rejoice, not suffer; but because it has been subjected to the servitude of corruption on our account, it suffers. For it sees its works perish daily; every day its work rises and sets. It therefore rightly suffers, whose activity pertains not to eternity but to corruption. As far as can be understood, they are sufficiently concerned for our salvation, knowing that their own liberation will come sooner if only we recognize our Creator more quickly. Knowing these things, therefore, let us show ourselves worthy of all care and diligence, so that we may also be an example to others, moved not only by compassion for ourselves, but also because of that creation which, suffering injuries day and night, groans; for we are accustomed to keep more watchfully watchful in the causes of others.

Romans 8:23 But not only that, but we ourselves who have the Spirit as our indwelling, we too groan within ourselves, awaiting the redemption of our body. 

After adding the worldly creation, Paul also added that we await this and groan to God, so that all destined for life may be freed from here; for to Christians this world is a sea. For just as the sea is stirred by contrary storms, surges, and creates a tempest for sailors, so the age, shaken by the conspiracy of the faithless, disturbs the minds of the faithful; and the enemy does this with such variety that one does not know what must first be avoided. For if the authorities cease to be against us, he agitates the minds of private individuals; if these too are restrained, he kindles a fire through household members; and if this is set aside, by his craft he creates discord among blood brothers themselves, so that, with the house battered at its four corners, he may cause its ruin from some side. Therefore Christians must flee from here with one purpose; for they ought to follow the example of holy Simeon, who, knowing that war was being waged here against faithlessness, asked to be dismissed in peace (Luke 2:29). This is what, each praying for all, we pray: that the kingdom of God may come (Matt. 6:10); for then there will be the liberation of our body, that is, of all Christians: by “body” he signified everyone, because we are members of one another. The Apostle, indeed, as a divine man, has a good understanding of us, saying that we groan so that we may be freed from here, just as Simeon also groaned; but I see that we are held by the allurements of the world. Therefore, through the things said above, Paul shows what suffering creation endures on our account; since we, for whom its service is beneficial and who have the Spirit of God as our helper, groan so that we may be freed from here and obtain the promised rewards. How much more, then, does creation groan, which neither has the Holy Spirit as a helper nor labors for its own sake, adding this, that it sees the things it produces through its labor, so that they may benefit the nourishment of God’s servants, being contrary to divine law sacrificed to idols! Therefore it grieves more and longs to be freed sooner, knowing that this contributes to the injury of the Creator.

Romans 8:24a For in hope we were saved. 

Paul says this because, by hoping for what God promised in Christ, we have earned the merit of being freed. Therefore we were freed in hope, because what is to come will be no different from what we believe.

Romans 8:24b Now hope that is seen is not hope. 

It is clear that what is seen is not hope, but what is not seen; and therefore believers are to receive rewards, because they hope for what they do not see.

Romans 8:25 For what one sees, what does one hope for? But if we hope for what we do not see, we await it with patience.

There is no doubt that what is not seen is hoped for; for the future is awaited. This expectation is patience, which merits much from God: that, awaiting the coming of God’s kingdom from day to day, one longs for it to come and does not doubt because it is delayed.

Romans 8:26a Likewise, the Spirit also helps the weakness of our prayer. 

For above Paul mentioned that we groan and pray for deliverance; and, placed in distress, we want either what is said to be far in the future to happen immediately, or to be quickly removed, though through these things we may attain merit; and we seem to pray with a weak prayer, while its effect is denied to us. For that prayer is weak which asks contrary to reason; and therefore he shows that this weakness is helped by the Holy Spirit given to us. He helps because he does not allow things to happen that are either requested before they ought to be requested or are harmful.

Romans 8:26b For we do not know what we should pray for as we ought. 

Paul made it clear that the weakness of our prayer signifies ignorance; for we are deceived when we think that what we ask for is beneficial, although it is not. Finally, when the same Apostle pleaded three times that the temptations might cease from him, because they were frequent, the Lord said to him: “My grace is sufficient for you; for power is made perfect in weakness” (2 Cor. 12:9), that is, one establishes merit in this way, when one is found patient in tribulations. Therefore he learned that what he was asking was against his own good. Sometimes a request is also proud and foolish, as was that of the two apostles James and John, who, when asking for something inappropriate and immense, were told: “You do not know what you are asking” (Matt. 20:22).

Romans 8:26c But the Spirit himself intercedes for us with inexpressible groanings. 

The Spirit of the Lord is not said to intercede for us in human words, but according to his nature. For when what is from God speaks to God, it must speak in the manner of the one from whom it is. No one speaks with his fellow citizen in another language. For the Spirit given to us pours itself over our prayers, so that by its movement it may cover our ignorance and lack of foresight, and ask God for those things that benefit us.

Romans 8:27 But he who searches the hearts knows what the Spirit desires, because he intercedes for the saints according to God’s will.

It is clear that to God, to whom nothing is silent or hidden, the prayer of all spirits is known; how much more that of the Holy Spirit, who is certainly of the same substance, and speaks not by the impulse of air, nor like the angels, nor like the other creatures, but as befits his divinity! Therefore he speaks to God when he seems silent to us; for he also sees when he is not seen, and asks for those things which he knows please God and benefit us. Indeed, that same Spirit intercedes for us when he knows that through ignorance, not presumption, we ask for things contrary to our good.

Romans 8:28 We know, however, that all things work together for good for those who love God, who are called saints according to his purpose. 

Paul says this because, even if those who love God have prayed unwisely, it will not harm them; for God, knowing the purpose of their hearts and their ignorance, does not ascribe to them the things they ask for that are harmful, but grants those that ought to be given to those who love God. Therefore the Lord also says: “For your Father knows what you need, before you ask him” (Matt. 6:32). Therefore those are called according to his purpose whom God foreknew, as believers, to be fit for himself, so that they might be known before they believed.

Romans 8:29a For those whom he foreknew. 

Those whom he foreknew as destined to be devoted to him, he chose to receive the promised rewards; so that those who seem to believe, yet do not remain in the faith they began, are denied as chosen by God; because those whom God has chosen remain with him. For one is chosen for a time, as Saul and Judas were; not according to foreknowledge, but according to present righteousness.

Romans 8:29b To be conformed to the image of his Son. 

Paul says this because they are predestined for the future age for this purpose: that they may become like the Son of God, as I have already mentioned above.

Romans 8:29c That he might be the firstborn among many brothers. 

Rightly called firstborn, for he was not made before all creation, but was born; after whose example God agreed to adopt human beings as sons. Christ is also the firstborn in regeneration. He is also the firstborn from the dead, whose nature is unknown. And the firstborn ascending into the heavens after victory. Therefore he is called our brother, firstborn in all things, because he agreed to be born a man; yet he is Lord, because he is our God, as the prophet Jeremiah says: “This is our God, and no other is reckoned beside him” (Baruch 3:36).

Romans 8:30a But those whom he predestined, he also called. 

To call is to assist one who is thinking about faith, or to prick the conscience of one whom he knows will listen.

Romans 8:30b And those whom he called, he also justified; and those whom he justified, he also glorified. 

Paul says this in keeping with what was stated above: those whom God foreknew to be suitable for him remain believers, because it cannot happen otherwise, unless those whom God foreknew, he also justified; and thereby he also glorified them, so that they might become like the Son of God. As for the others whom God did not foreknow, he has no concern for them in this grace, because he did not foreknow them as destined to be worthy. But if they believe, or are chosen for a time because they seem good, lest justice appear to be disregarded, they do not remain so as to be glorified, as with Judas Iscariot, or those seventy-two who, after suffering scandal, withdrew from the Savior, though they had been chosen (Jn. 6:71).

Romans 8:31 What then shall we say to these things? If God is for us, who can be against us? 

It is clear that, with God bearing witness for us, who would dare accuse us, when he himself who is the judge foreknew us and judged us worthy?

Romans 8:32a He who did not spare His own Son, but delivered Him up for us all. 

Therefore Paul encourages us to be secure in faith, showing that God, as if foreknowing, before we became faithful from being ungodly, delivered His Son to death for us.

Romans 8:32b How will He not also give us all things with Him? 

Paul says that God once decreed that those who believe in Christ should be rewarded. For if He granted us what is greater and foremost, delivering His true and beloved Son for our sake while we were still ungodly, why should it not be believed that He will do what is less for us who believe in Him? The rewards are already prepared for believers; for it is less to give us all things with Him than to deliver Him to death for our sake.

Romans 8:33a Who shall bring any accusation against God’s elect? 

It is clear that no one dares or is able to reconsider God’s judgment and foreknowledge concerning us. For who could disapprove what God approves, since no one is equal to God?

Romans 8:33b It is God who justifies (Isa. 50:8).

This is found in the prophet Isaiah, which Paul here presents as his own; because there is no one else who can condemn what God approves. Or perhaps God himself might accuse us, but he cannot accuse those whom he justifies.

Romans 8:34 And who will condemn? Christ who died, indeed, who also rose again, and is at the right hand of God, who also intercedes for us. 

He denies that we can accuse God, because He justifies us; but that we cannot condemn Christ, because He loves us with such affection that He died for us and, rising again, always pleads our causes before the Father. His petition cannot be disregarded, because He is at the right hand of God, that is, in the honor that belongs to God; so that, secure in God the Father and in Christ His Son, who will judge, we may rejoice in His faith. Therefore is that which He says to the apostle Peter: Behold, Satan has demanded to sift you as wheat; but I have prayed for you, that your faith may not fail (Luke 22:31-32). In this way, therefore, the Savior intercedes for us; for knowing the shamelessness and arrogance of our adversary, when he rises up against us, if we do not consent to him, the Savior intercedes for us, lest he presume to do anything violently against us, so that the adversary’s arrogance may be restrained. For this reason, however, the Son is said to intercede, although He Himself accomplishes everything and is equal to God the Father; and because God is said to be one, lest the Father and the Son be thought to be a single person or a unity, Scripture speaks in this way to distinguish the persons; so that it presents the Son as not unequal, and gives precedence to the Father, because He is the Father and because all things are from Him.

Romans 8:35 Who shall separate us from the love of Christ? 

That is, who can turn us away from the love of Christ, who has bestowed upon us such great and countless benefits? Tribulation? But it does not, because no torments can overcome the love of a steadfast Christian. For the love of one who loves is stirred up all the more if he is forbidden to love the one whose benefits he has experienced; for while they think they are sowing into his merits, they recall the benefits received.

Romans 8:36-37 Is it anguish? Is it persecution? Is it hunger? Is it nakedness? Is it danger? Is it the sword (as it is written, “Because for your sake we are put to death all day long; we are regarded as sheep for slaughter”)? Yet in all these things we conquer through him who loved us. 

This is written in the forty-third Psalm. It is therefore clear that all these things he lists, destruction, tribulations, afflictions, and death, cannot be compared or equated with the love of Christ, which he has sown in us. For we have benefits far greater than all these things that seem adverse. For if we die for him, which appears the most grievous of these things, he too died for us, but he died to benefit us; whereas our death does not benefit him, but us. For we lose temporal life so that eternal life may be repaid to us. And what wonder if servants die for a good Lord, when the Lord died for his servants, even the wicked? His benefits therefore prevail, and they encourage the spirit to persevere for the sake of him who loved us.

Romans 8:38a For I am confident that neither death. 

This confidence concerns Christ’s promise, by which he promised to help the faith devoted to him in tribulation.

Romans 8:38b-39 Neither life, nor angel, nor power, nor height, nor depth, nor things present, nor things to come, nor any other creature will be able to separate us from the love of God, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord. 

These are all the things brought forward for our abduction by the Devil, which Paul mentions for this reason: to fortify us, so that, if they come, trusting in hope and in Christ’s aids, armed with faith, we may resist them. For what if death is inflicted? Is it not the greatest gain to find an occasion by which one may go more quickly into the promised kingdom? Nor, if this present life should be promised to us, fortified by honor, ought it to turn us away from hope in Christ’s benefit, since we know that he will profit us not only in the future but also in the present. Nor indeed, if an angel should show himself to us in order to deceive us, prompted by the deceptions of his father the Devil, ought he to prevail against us, since we know that nothing should be preferred to Christ, the Angel of the great counsel (Isa. 9:6). Nor, if some power should be performed by anyone, as is said to have been done by Simon Magus, who is said to have flown upward in the air to scandalize Christ’s people, ought it to diminish our faith, since we know that the Savior, borne up by a serving cloud, ascended above all the heavens (Acts 1:9). Nor, if he should display himself to us in height, concerning which the apostle John says, “Or do you not know the depth of Satan?” (Rev. 2:24), ought he to remove us from devotion to the Lord Jesus, whom we know descended from heaven to unite earthly things with spiritual ones. Nor, if through a ghost by which he plans to deceive us he should show us a depth dreadful to behold, by which we might perhaps be terrified and submit to him, is it right even then for us to break faith with Christ, whom we know descended into the depths of the earth for our sake and, by the pressure of death, liberated the human race. Nor, if he should promise us things to come, which he promised Eve (Gen. 3:4), will we give him our assent, having been separated from Christ, whom we believe and know to be God in power and nature. Nor, if by the art and subtleness of his cunning he should for a time fabricate another creature, as Jannes and Jambres fabricated before Pharaoh, is it fitting that through these things he should recall us from the true Creator God, whom we know to have created through Christ his Son a creature existing forever (2 Tim. 3:8). Some think that he said “another creature” concerning idols; but this is not true, because he must have meant that which Satan appears to fabricate under the guise of wondrous deception. For who among the faithful would be deceived by these things, from which they withdrew once the error was revealed? But he devises and fabricates these things by which he might even deceive the elect. Therefore, nothing can separate us from the love of God, which is in Christ Jesus: for God showed us his love in Christ when he gave him up for us.

Chapter 9

Romans 9:1-3 I speak the truth in Christ Jesus, I do not lie, my conscience bearing witness with me in the Holy Spirit, that I have great sorrow and continual pain in my heart; for I myself could wish to be accursed and separated from Christ for the sake of my brethren, my kinsmen according to the flesh, who are Israelites.

Since above Paul seems to speak against the Jews, who think they are justified by the Law, now, to show his wish and affection toward them, he says, with his conscience bearing witness, that he speaks in Christ Jesus and in the Holy Spirit, so that his faith may not be regarded as impossible because he is their enemy. Therefore he gives Christ and the Holy Spirit, from whom nothing is hidden, as witnesses; their testimony cannot be rejected, and they therefore bear witness to the Apostle while commending him through the power of the signs they performed through him. For because Christ is such a great protection, has shown special love toward the human race, and through his glorious condescension promised immortal rewards, he grieves for his own people, who are his kin according to the flesh; because by their unbelief they deprived themselves of this perpetual and saving benefit. Therefore, he says, “I could wish,” not “I wish,” because he knows it is impossible that so honorable a member, without any preceding fault, should be cut off from the Christian body; nevertheless, he shows his affection and love toward his own people.

Romans 9:4 Their adoption is that of sons. 

Paul proves himself to be rightly grieved when he proclaims the praise of their lineage, because formerly the adopted sons had the affection and grace of God the Father in vain. He further added that he might make others grieve for them also, saying:

Romans 9:5a And the glory, and the covenants, and the legislation, and the worship, and the promises, whose are the Fathers, and from whom Christ is according to the flesh, who is God over all, blessed forever. Amen. 

Paul reckons so many praises of the nobility and dignity of the Jewish race and of the promises that he might instill grief in everyone over these things; because, by not receiving the Savior, they lost the privilege of the Fathers and the merit of the promise, becoming worse than the Gentiles, whom previously, because they were without God, they hated: for it is worse to have lost dignity than never to have had it. Meanwhile, concerning the Savior he says:

Romans 9:5b Who is God over all, blessed forever. Amen. 

For when no mention is made of the Father’s name, and the discourse concerns Christ, it cannot be otherwise than that he is called God. Frequently, indeed, Scripture, because of its confession of one God, when it speaks of God the Father and joins the Son to God, calls the Father God and the Son Lord. But if anyone does not think that this was said of Christ: Who is God, let him identify the person of whom it was said: for no mention of God the Father has been made in this passage. But what wonder if in this passage he openly spoke of Christ as God over all, of whom elsewhere in the epistle Paul confirmed this meaning in such words, saying: That at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth (Phil. 2:10)? These are all things over which Christ is God. For there are no others, apart from these, so that Christ is not God over all; nor can every creature bow the knee except to God. Finally, when the apostle John, unknowingly wishing to worship an angel as God, heard from him: Do not do this, for I am your fellow servant. Worship God, he says (Rev.. 19:10). Nor, certainly, would the Lord allow himself to be worshiped unless he were God; otherwise he would have to be accused of trespass and sin, which is impossible; since he himself, rebuking the Devil, shows that the Lord God is to be worshiped and that he alone is to be served (Matt. 4:10). Therefore nothing is unjust against God the Father when Christ is worshiped as God; because, although it is said that only God is to be served, Christ too is served. For he says elsewhere: Whoever serves Christ in these things is pleasing to God (Jn. 10:4). What, then, remains except that the Father is God and the Son is God, and nevertheless both are believed to be one God? For whether one worships the Father or the Son, he is said to worship one God; and whether one serves the Father or the Son, service is rendered to one God. Therefore there is no distinction; because whoever worships the Son also worships the Father, and whoever serves the Father also serves the Son. And to show that this confession of deity was without flattery, he placed Amen at the conclusion, that is, true, so that he might show Christ, God over all, to be truly blessed forever.

Romans 9:6 But the word of God has not failed. The word of God has not failed when he said: “In Isaac shall your offspring be named” (Gen. 21:12).

That is, what God said would happen has happened; so that those who were his children according to the flesh would not be called the offspring of Abraham, but rather those who have received the faith through which Isaac was born, renewed in the time of Christ, no longer particular but universal; so that what Abraham believed concerning Isaac, they would believe concerning God and Christ, because the Son of God was born to redeem the salvation of the human race.

Romans 9:7 Not all who are descended from Israel are Israelites; nor, because they are Abraham’s offspring, are they all children: but, “In Isaac shall your offspring be called.” (Gen. 21:12)

This means that not all are thereby considered worthy because they are Abraham’s children, but those are worthy who are children of the promise, that is, those whom God foreknew would receive his promise, whether from the Jews or from the Gentiles. For these deserve to be called Israelites, that is, those who see God, who believe. For all these are certainly children of Abraham through Isaac, since the entire lineage of the Jewish people descends from Abraham through Isaac. But because, as I said, those are truly Abraham’s children who follow the promise made in Isaac, the remaining children should not be called Abraham’s. For Abraham, believing, received Isaac because of his faith, because he believed God. In this, the mystery of future faith was foreshadowed: that those would be Isaac’s brothers who would have the same faith in which Isaac was born, because Isaac was born as a type of the Savior from the promise, so that whoever believed that Jesus Christ had been promised to Abraham would be Abraham’s child and Isaac’s brother. Therefore it was said to Abraham, “In your offspring all nations shall be blessed” (Gen. 22:18). This was certainly not fulfilled in Isaac, but in him who was promised to Abraham through Isaac, namely Christ, in whom all believing nations are blessed. The other Jews, therefore, are children of the flesh, while being deprived of the promise; nor can they be reckoned worthy on account of Abraham, since they do not follow the faith by which Abraham became worthy.

Romans 9:8 That is, not those who are children of the flesh are children of God, but those who are children of the promise are counted as offspring. 

It is clear that children of the flesh cannot be called children of God: for they were born from the desire of the flesh; but these were born spiritually through faith, according to what was promised to Abraham, so that those who believed might be considered offspring.

Romans 9:9 For this is the word of the promise: “At this time I will come, and Sarah shall have a son.” (Gen. 18:10)

This is found in Genesis, prefigured in Christ, so that Christ, who was to come, might be promised as the son of Abraham, in whom the word of the promise would be fulfilled, so that all the nations of the earth would be blessed in Christ. For when the promise was made to Abraham, and he heard, “In your offspring all the nations of the earth shall be blessed,” (Gen. 22:18) Christ was surely promised to him through the lineage of Isaac, in whom we see this fulfilled.

Romans 9:10 Not only this one, that is, Sarah, but also Rebecca, having Isaac our father from one union. 

Therefore Paul does not say that only Sarah, in the typological sense, gave birth to Isaac, but also Rebecca, Isaac’s wife. But one matter concerns Isaac, and another Jacob and Esau; for Isaac was born as a figure of the Savior, whereas Jacob and Esau represent two peoples, that is, believers and unbelievers, so that although they are from one, they are nevertheless different. For in each case a kind is signified, so that those are said to be of one kind who are united in faith or in unbelief. For one is signified in many, not through carnal descent, but through a common cause; because the sons of Jacob are also to be reckoned among Esau, and the sons of Esau among Jacob. For neither, because Jacob is praised, should all born from him rightly be called his sons; nor, because Esau is displeasing, are all descended from him reprobate, since we see both the treacherous born from Jacob’s stock and the faithful and beloved of God from Esau. For that many of Jacob’s descendants are dangerous is beyond doubt; all the unbelieving Jews, or the believers, derive their origin from him. And that there are good and faithful men descended from Esau is proved by Job, who is the fifth from Abraham among the sons of Esau, that is, Esau’s grandson.

Romans 9:11-13 For before they had been born, or had done anything good or evil, so that God’s purpose according to election might remain, it was said, not from works, but from him who calls, “The elder shall serve the younger,” (Gen. 25:23) as it is written: “Jacob I loved, but Esau I hated.” (Mal. 1:2–3)

This is found in Malachi. He demands God’s foreknowledge in these matters, since nothing can happen other than what God knows will happen. For knowing what each of them would become, he said: “This one, who will be the younger, will be worthy; and the elder will be unworthy.” By his foreknowledge he chose one and rejected the other; and in the one whom he chose, God’s purpose remains, because nothing can happen other than what he knew and purposed concerning him, that he should be worthy of salvation; and in the one whom he rejected, likewise remains the purpose he had formed concerning him, because he would be unworthy. This is the conduct of one who foreknows, not of one who shows partiality; for he condemns no one before he sins, nor crowns anyone before he conquers. This concerns the case of the Jews, who claim for themselves the privilege of being Abraham’s children. The Apostle, however, consoles himself: since he had said that he had continual sorrow in his heart because of the unbelief of those whose adoption as sons, covenant, and from whom Christ the Savior came, just as he himself says, “Salvation is from the Jews” (Jn. 4:22), after examining the Law, he found that not all who are of Israel would believe; nor, because they are called Abraham’s children, should all be called Abraham’s children, as I mentioned above. Therefore he lessens his sorrow, finding that it had long ago been foretold that not all would believe, so that he grieves only for those who through envy struggle in unbelief. Yet they can believe, as he makes clear from what follows. But one should not grieve greatly for these unbelievers, since they were not predestined to life; for God’s foreknowledge long ago decreed that they would not be saved. Who, after all, mourns for one regarded as long since dead? But when the Gentiles, who previously were without God, enter and receive the salvation that the Jews lost, sorrow is revived; yet again, because they themselves are the cause of their destruction, it is quieted. Therefore God, foreknowing that they would have an evil will, did not count them among the good, although the Savior says to the seventy-two disciples whom he had chosen in the second group, and who later departed from him: “Your names are written in heaven” (Luke 10:20). But this was according to justice, because it is just that each person receive according to his merit. Since they were good, they were chosen for ministry; and their names were written in heaven because of justice, as I said. But according to foreknowledge they were counted among the wicked. For God judges according to justice, not according to foreknowledge. Therefore he also said to Moses: “If anyone has sinned against me, I will blot him out of my book” (Exod. 32, 34), so that according to the justice of the Judge he appears to be blotted out when he sins; but according to foreknowledge, he had never been in the book of life. Therefore the Apostle John also says of such people: “They went out from us, but they were not of us; for if they had been of us, they would certainly have remained with us” (I Jn. 2:20). There is no partiality in God’s foreknowledge; for foreknowledge is that by which he has determined what each person’s future will shall be, in which he will remain, and by which he will either be condemned or crowned. Indeed, those whom he knows will remain in goodness are often wicked beforehand; and those whom he knows will remain wicked are sometimes good beforehand. Therefore complaint ceases, because God is not one who shows partiality (Acts 10:34). For Saul and Judas Iscariot were also good at first. Scripture says of Saul: “He was a good man, and there was none better among the children of Israel” (1 Sam. 9:2); and concerning Judas Iscariot the Apostle Peter says: “He obtained a share in this ministry” (Acts 1:17), that is, the apostleship, in performing signs and wonders. How, then, could he have obtained a saving ministry unless he had been good? In the casting of lots, God’s judgment showed that he was worthy at the time when he was chosen, like the seventy-two whom I mentioned above (Matt. 17:5). That is why Judas, after committing every evil crime, moved by repentance, ended his life by hanging. For all goodness cannot be completely obliterated in anyone, since nature cannot be changed, but the will cannot, however, in every circumstance; for something remains in nature that bears witness to the Creator.

Romans 9:14 What then shall we say? Is there injustice with God? By no means. 

For because He loves one and hates the other, he adds: Is there injustice with God (Exod. 33:19)? Certainly not, but God is just; for He knows what He does, and His judgment is not to be withdrawn. This is found in the prophet Malachi: “Jacob I loved, but Esau I hated” (Mal. 1:3). Here he is already speaking of judgment; for earlier he speaks of foreknowledge, saying that the elder shall serve the younger, and likewise, by His foreknowledge, He condemned Pharaoh (Gen. 25:23), knowing that he would not be corrected; but He chose the apostle Paul, who was persecuting Him (Acts 9:3), knowing beforehand, of course, that he would become good. Therefore He anticipated him before the appointed time, because he was necessary; and He condemned Pharaoh before the future judgment, so that He might be believed to be the Judge.

Romans 9:15 For he says to Moses: “I will have mercy on whom I have mercy, and I will show compassion to whom I show compassion.” 

Therefore, “I will have mercy,” he says, on the one on whom I will have mercy, that is, I will have mercy on the one whom I foreknew would receive mercy, knowing that he would turn and remain with me. “And I will show compassion to him on whom I show compassion,” that is, I will give mercy to the one whom I foreknew would return to me with a sincere heart after his error. This means giving to the one to whom it ought to be given, and not giving to the one to whom it ought not to be given: calling the one whom he knows will obey, but not calling the one whom he knows will by no means obey. To call, however, is not to compel, but to pierce the heart so that it may receive faith.

Romans 9:16 Therefore it is not of him who wills or runs, but of God who shows mercy. 

Rightly so, because what is requested should depend not on the will of the one asking, but on the decision of the one giving. For whether it should be given must be weighed by the judgment of the giver; for when Saul sinned and asked forgiveness, he did not receive it (1 Kings 15:24); whereas David, having sinned and asked to be forgiven, obtained pardon (2 Sam. 12:8). Certainly, then, the judgment of God, who gives and does not give, must be followed; for he does not judge unjustly, who wishes all to be saved while justice remains intact. For he who examines the heart knows whether the petitioner asks with such a disposition that he deserves to receive. And although it is dangerous to discern God’s judgment, nevertheless, for the sake of those who doubt, so that their minds may obtain healing and they may not think God’s judgment unjust, saying, “He calls one and neglects another,” and therefore imagining that the condemned can be excused, let us prove this by facts rather than words. For where there are examples of deeds, no one dares complain or offer any excuse. There were two men, David and Saul. Let us examine their histories and see how each of them was found after God’s judgment, so that if Saul is proved to have acted well after not receiving mercy, we may, God forbid, consider God’s judgment unjust; or if David, having received mercy, is found to have despised God, whether he remained in the state in which he obtained mercy. To put it briefly, both suffered the loss of their kingdom. And how great was David’s distress, when his son wanted to deprive him of the kingdom! In that distress he traveled weeping and barefoot, fleeing as king and leader of God’s people, humbled so greatly that he did not even answer his servant who cursed him to his face, so that through patience he might make God favorable to him, through whom he believed the kingdom would be preserved for him (2 Sam. 15:16). But Saul was not found in such distress, because civil war is a greater evil than external war. Moreover, although he had been refused once and again, he did not persist in prayer, though unworthy, so as to earn merit by which he might become worthy. Instead, impatient and indignant at God’s judgment, he sought help from the idols he had previously condemned as worthless. Behold, the justice of God’s foreknowledge is revealed even to those unwilling to accept it.

Romans 9:17 For Scripture says to Pharaoh (Exod. 9:16): “For this very purpose I have preserved you, that I may show my power in you, and that my name may be proclaimed throughout all the earth.” 

Other manuscripts read: “For this purpose I raised you up, that I may show my power in you.” Whether “I preserved” or “I raised up,” the meaning is the same. For it says that when Pharaoh, for that was the name of the kings among the Egyptians, just as among the Romans kings are called Augusti, was guilty of such great evils that he was unworthy of life, he nevertheless persisted in his wickedness and refused to improve, thinking either that he deserved to live or that God, whom he often supposed could be deceived, was powerless to inflict punishment. He hears from God: “For this purpose I have preserved you, that I may show my power in you, and that my name may be proclaimed throughout all the earth,” so that other nations might learn that there is no other God besides this one, who belonged to the Jews and became the God of Christians; although they too were Christians, and we are now Jews because of Judah, from whom Christ is descended according to the flesh; for the Jews of old, hoping for Christ the Redeemer who was to come, were Christians. For Rahab the prostitute says to the spies sent by Jesus son of Nave, who was previously called Auxes: “We have heard here of the mighty deeds and plagues that were formed in Egypt by your God, and we were greatly terrified; for they fear before your presence” (Josh. 2:9). Pharaoh, therefore, was preserved for this purpose, so that many signs and plagues might be displayed upon him as though he were already dead. He is said to have been raised up because, although he was dead before God, he was granted a short time so that he might appear to live; so that, seeing him subjected to punishment and varied kinds of torment until death, all who were without God, terrified by fear, might confess with the greatest awe that this alone was God, from whom these punishments came. In this same way the ancient physicians, in the case of men deserving death or condemned to death, sought to discover how they might benefit the living by opening what lay hidden within the human body; therefore they learned the causes of illness, and the punishment of the dying contributed to the salvation of the living.

Romans 9:18 Therefore He has mercy on whom He wills, and whom He wills He hardens. You will say to me, then. 

He speaks in the person of one who objects, who seems to think that God, disregarding justice, is gracious to someone; so that He accepts one of two equals and rejects the other, that is, He pierces the heart of one, so that he may believe, and hardens the other, lest he believe. He answered him with authority, while nevertheless preserving justice, saying on the basis of the foreknowledge mentioned above:

Romans 9:19 What more is there to ask? For who can resist his will? 

First, he says that he cannot be resisted, for he is more powerful than all. Then, because he is the God and Father of all, and therefore wishes evil upon no one; for he wants the things he has made to remain unharmed. Nor is it fitting that he should be unjust, whose benevolence appears so great that he not only brought into existence things that did not exist, but also granted those very things eternal life and glory, so that his work might possess something resembling his majesty. Therefore, since he is so provident and good, there should be no doubt that he is just.

Romans 9:20 O man, who are you to answer God! 

Great, he says, is the indignity and presumption of a man answering God in adversity, the unjust answering the just, the evil the good, the ignorant the perfect, the weak the strongest, the corruptible the incorruptible, the mortal the immortal, the worthless servant the lord, the creature the creator. Does the thing formed say to him who formed it: “Why have you made me therefore?” (Isa. 45:9). This is from the prophet Isaiah, which he sets down here as his own, showing that a work cannot complain against its author; for it is in the author’s power to create whatever kind of creature he wishes.

Romans 9:21 Does not the potter have power over the clay, from the same lump to make one vessel for honor, and another for dishonor?

It is clear that some vessels are made for honor, being necessary for honorable uses; others, however, for dishonor, being utensils for the kitchen; yet they are of one substance, differing only in the craftsman’s intention regarding their honor. So also God, since we are all of one and the same substance, and all sinners, has mercy on one and despises another, not without justice. For in the potter there is only intention; but in God, intention together with justice, for he knows whom he ought to pity, as I mentioned above.

Romans 9:22 But if God, willing to show his wrath and to make known his power, endured with much patience vessels of wrath prepared for destruction. 

The meaning itself is that, by the will and long-suffering of God, which is patience, unbelievers are prepared for punishment; for, having long been awaited, they refused to convert. Therefore they were awaited, so that they might perish without excuse. For God knew that they would not believe.

Romans 9:23 And to show the riches of his glory in the vessels of mercy, which he prepared for glory. 

The patience and forbearance of God itself is what prepares the wicked for destruction, just as it prepares the good for the crown; for the good are those in whom there is the hope of faith. For he sustains all, knowing the outcome of each one; and therefore it is patience that prepares for glory those who are corrected from evil, or who persevere in goodness. The riches of glory, moreover, are the diverse dignity prepared for believers. But those who become wicked from being good, and persist in the evil they have begun, he prepares for destruction. This, then, is to reveal his power in great patience: because he is considered not to be going to avenge, since he delays for a long time; when he begins to avenge, his power will appear; for although he can avenge immediately, he endured for a long time, so that the faithless cannot complain when they are condemned. But to prepare each one is to know beforehand what will happen.

Romans 9:24 Those whom he also called us, not only from the Jews but also from the Gentiles. 

Those whom he called, he prepared for glory, both those who were near and those who were far away, knowing that they would remain in the faith.

Romans 9:25-26 As it says in Hosea: I will call those who were not my people, my people; and her who was not beloved, beloved. And it shall be in the place where they were called not my people; there they shall be called sons of the living God. 

It is clear that this was foretold concerning the Gentiles, who previously were not God’s people: afterward, however, receiving mercy to the criticism of the Jews, they were called God’s people;   and those who previously were not beloved, after the Jews were rejected, were adopted as sons and became beloved; so that in the place where they were not called God’s people, there they might be called sons of the living God. For previously they were nowhere called sons of God except in Judea, that is, Jerusalem, where the house of God was, as it says in the seventy-fifth Psalm: God is known in Judea (Ps. 75:2). Afterwards, however, in the prophecy of Zechariah: I will set Jerusalem, he says, among all the nations (Zech. 12:2); because sons of God were to be everywhere, and the house of God in all places, which is the Church. Therefore the Lord says to the Jews: For the kingdom of God will be taken away from you and given to a nation producing its fruits (Matt. 21:43).

Romans 9:27a But Isaiah cries out for Israel. 

Paul says this because Isaiah cries out for those who believe in Christ (Isa. 10:22). For these are truly Israelites, as the Lord says to Nathanael: “Behold, truly an Israelite, in whom there is no deceit” (Jn. 1:47).

Romans 9:27b Though the number of the children of Israel be as the sand of the sea, a remnant will be saved. 

As the Jews withdraw from the merit and promise of the fathers by not accepting it, these are the remnant who persist by believing in the faith of the promise made to the fathers, having become faithful; for they withdraw from the Law who do not believe in him whom the Law promised alone to be sufficient for salvation. Therefore, they must necessarily be regarded as apostates, who, by not accepting Christ, are guilty of violating the Law. Therefore, from the very great multitude, Paul says that only the believers, whom God foreknew, will be saved.

Romans 9:28 For He will finish and cut short His word in righteousness; for the Lord will make a shortened word upon the earth. 

He promises to save these whom He calls the remnant through the word, which, defining it justly, the Lord has shortened upon the earth; for it is fitting that the creature should attain salvation in the name alone of the Lord the Creator, that is, through faith; because, once all the new moons, the Sabbath, circumcision, the law of foods, and offerings of animals were removed, faith alone was established for salvation. This is what has been abbreviated from the Law; for what is in faith is contained in the Law as the principal thing of the Law, as the Savior says: “For Moses wrote about me” (Jn. 5:46). Therefore, with the Law abbreviated, the remnant of the Jews are saved; but the others cannot be saved, because they are rejected by God’s decree, through which He determined to save the human race.

Romans 9:29 And as Isaiah foretold: Unless the Lord of hosts had left us a seed, we would have been like Sodom, and we would have become like Gomorrah (Isa. 1:9).

This seed, which alone was left and preserved from all for the restoration of the human race, is Christ, and his teaching, just as he himself says: The seed is the word of God (Luke 8:11). This, therefore, once promised to us, after removing the things that were burdensome in the Law, he left for redemption; so that, forgiveness of sins having been granted, we would not be punished by the Law, as Sodom perished. Paul therefore says that the Savior was left to us as an aid to life, which the Law could not provide, whom God decreed from the beginning would be born: because he alone was found without sin, he would destroy all sins, having conquered the enemy of the human race. This is also found in the Apocalypse of John: Because no one, he says, was found worthy, either in heaven or on earth, to open the book and its seals, except the Savior, who conquered death (Rev. 5:3). This, therefore, is the seed that God once promised and preserved, so that it might bear fruit when the sins of all, both Jews and Gentiles, had reached their fullness. Its fruit, however, is the forgiveness of sins; for unless Christ had been preserved (whom he therefore calls seed, because through him the human race was restored), the offspring of Abraham would have perished, since they were overwhelmed by sins, and the Law could not benefit them. Therefore he teaches that this one should be followed, who brought the protection of life.

Romans 9:30 What then shall we say? That the Gentiles, who did not pursue righteousness, attained righteousness: the righteousness, however, that comes from faith. 

Since there is a righteousness in the Law, which was entrusted to the world, which forbids sinning; therefore he does not say that the Gentiles attained this righteousness, which is recognized by nature as teacher, but that righteousness which comes from faith in Christ; for this is true and enduring righteousness with God, if it is acknowledged. For what is so righteous as to know God the Father, from whom all things are, and Christ his Son, through whom all things are (1 Cor. 8:6)? Therefore the first righteousness is to acknowledge the Creator, and then to keep what he commanded. The Gentiles, therefore, who previously did not seek righteousness, that is, the Law, which bore witness to the Creator, when Christ came, found a righteousness surpassing that of the scribes and Pharisees. For those who then did not pursue what was lesser attained afterward what was greater; but the Jews, placed under the Law, who ought to have advanced more, grew worse.

Romans 9:31 For Israel, pursuing the Law of righteousness, did not attain the Law of righteousness. 

The perfection of the Law is faith, and the Gentiles, embracing it, seem to fulfill the whole Law. But the Jews, refusing to believe in the Savior out of envy, while claiming the righteousness commanded in the Law, namely, the Sabbath, circumcision, and so forth, did not attain the Law, that is, they did not perfect the Law; and those who do not perfect the Law are guilty according to the Law. For he fulfills the Law who, from the law of Moses, comes to faith in Christ.

Romans 9:32 Why? Because it was not from faith, but as if from works: 

for, scorning the faith which, as I said, is the perfection of the Law, they claimed to be justified by the works of the Law, that is, by the Sabbath, new moons, circumcision, etc., forgetful of the Scripture which says, “The just shall live by faith” (Hab. 2:4). The righteousness of the Law is so called because, by God’s just judgment, these things were given to the Jews on account of the hardness of their hearts; so that if someone happened to step on a dead weasel, or touch some carcass, or if a shrew fell into a vessel, it was declared unclean (Lev. 11:29): observing these things with great carefulness, they became unclean for any cause. But if the blood of a weasel stained the pavement, it had to be cleansed with no little care, and one had to abstain from better foods. The Sabbath, however, and circumcision had their own righteousness in their time, because they were given as figures. The Lord showed this through the prophet Ezekiel, saying, among other things: “Therefore I gave them statutes that were not good” (Ezek. 20:25); because they were irreverent and faithless. But at Christ’s coming, since He was to give the gift of salvation, He proclaims through Jeremiah, saying: “I will give them a new covenant, not like the one I gave their fathers” (Jer. 31:32). He calls this covenant the Law, which they did not attain, as I mentioned above. For when the believing Jews wished to impose this burden of observance upon the Gentiles who had believed, the Apostle Peter then said: “Why do you place a yoke upon the necks of the brethren which neither we nor our fathers have been able to bear?” (Acts 15:10). For from the time of Christ, the forgiveness promised in the Law was granted; for the prophet Isaiah says: “He will come from Zion, who will deliver and turn away ungodliness from Jacob. And this is my covenant with them, when I take away their sins” (Isa. 59:20), that is, the new covenant promised by God in Christ.

Romans 9:33 They stumbled over the stone of stumbling, as it is written: “Behold, I place in Zion a stone of stumbling and a rock of scandal; and whoever believes in him will not be put to shame.”

This is found in Isaiah (Isa. 8:14; 28:6). It has been established by the testimony of many that Christ is signified by the rock or stone; for the prophet Daniel calls this the stone which, cut from the mountain without hands, struck and shattered all kingdoms and filled the whole earth, something evidently spoken of Christ (Dan. 2:34). And in the Law the rock from which waters flowed is called Christ: “The rock,” he says, “was Christ.” And the apostle Peter, among other things, said to the Jews: “This is the stone which was rejected by you builders” (1 Cor. 10:4). Therefore, the stone of stumbling placed in Zion is Christ (Acts 4:11). Zion, however, is a height, or the city of Jerusalem itself, which was rightly called lofty because of its knowledge of God; in it the Savior, appointed preacher by God his Father, became a stumbling block to the Jews while proclaiming himself the Son of God, born of a woman by the Holy Spirit. Through his body they suffered scandal, and they said: “Are not his mother and brothers with us? How then does he say: ‘Because I have come down from heaven’” (Jn. 6:42)? For they did not wish to compare his words with his deeds, so as thereby to recognize that what he said about having come down from heaven was not absurd, as though the body were acting, and not God, hidden yet revealing himself through his works in the body. Therefore this rock is a scandal and stumbling block to the Jews: this rock undoubtedly signifies the Savior’s body. It was cut without hands because it was formed from a virgin by the Holy Spirit, without a man.

Chapter 10

Romans 10:1-2 Brothers, the desire of my heart and my prayer to God for them is for their salvation; for I bear them witness that they have zeal for God, but not according to knowledge. 

Since Paul wishes to remove them from the Law, believing that it is a veil for the Jews, lest he seem to do this out of hatred for Judaism, he shows his affection for them and gives great weight to the Law; but he teaches that the time for keeping the Law has now passed. Therefore he testifies that he wishes to provide for them, if only they will listen to him and recognize that he is not their enemy; for he also bears witness to the nobility of their ancestral tradition.

Romans 10:3 For, being ignorant of the righteousness of God and seeking to establish their own, they are not subject to the righteousness of God. 

By “ignorance” Paul says that they did not believe in Christ; although they had zeal for God, not knowing his will and purpose, they acted against God, whom they claimed to defend. He speaks of those who did not receive Christ, not through hatred and envy, but through error. To them the Apostle Peter also says: “I know, brothers, that you did this evil through ignorance, as did your rulers” (Acts 3:17); for, being ignorant that this was the Christ whom God had promised, they said that another was to be expected. Preferring to him their own righteousness, which they had from the Law, he is the righteousness of God through faith. For he himself is righteousness, because God fulfilled in him what he had promised.

Romans 10:4 For Christ is the end of the Law for righteousness to everyone who believes. 

Paul says this because whoever believes in Christ possesses the perfection of the Law. Since no one was justified by the Law, because no one fulfilled the Law except one who hoped in the promised Christ, faith was established to take the place of the perfection of the Law, so that, with everything else set aside, faith might serve for the whole Law and the prophets.

Romans 10:5 For Moses writes of the righteousness that is from the Law, “The person who does these things will live by them” (Lev. 18:5).

Paul says this because the righteousness of Moses’ law did not make those guilty for a time, if it was observed; that is, they lived by doing the Law, for they were debtors. This is stated in Numbers and in Leviticus.

Romans 10:6a But the righteousness that comes from faith speaks therefore: “Do not say in your heart: Who has ascended into heaven?” (Deut. 30: 12).

This was said in Deuteronomy, which the Apostle here interprets concerning Christ, saying:

Romans 10:6b-7 “That is, to bring Christ down.” Or, “Who has descended into the abyss?” That is, “to bring Christ back from the dead.” 

This is proper to the Apostle. Therefore he says that this righteousness belongs to faith, if there is no doubt about the hope of God, which is in Christ; lest anyone, distrusting, say: “Who could ascend into heaven?” For he suffered for this reason: that, having stripped the underworld [inferis] by the power of the Father, and conquered death, he might rise again and ascend into heaven with the souls he had rescued. For everyone who, upon seeing the Savior in the underworld [inferos], hoped for salvation from him was delivered, as the Apostle Peter testifies; for he says that “the gospel was preached even to the dead” (1 Pet. 4:6). Whoever, therefore, does not doubt these things in his heart is justified by faith. But fear causes one to be justified by the law; for he fears the law because he sees that it inflicts punishment on sinners. Therefore the righteousness of the law is not great, nor does it confer merit before God, but only for the present; whereas faith, though foolishness to unbelievers, has a reward with God, from whom what is not seen is hoped for.

Romans 10:8 But what does Scripture say? The word is near you, in your mouth and in your heart (Deut. 30:14).

This is written in Deuteronomy because what is said to us so that we may believe is not far from our mind or mouth; for although it is not seen by the eyes, what we believe is nevertheless not contrary to the nature of souls and the manner of speaking. Indeed, seeds are implanted in that very nature, which, cultivated by hearing and the will, bear fruit as a testimony to the Creator.

Romans 10:9a This is the word of faith that we preach. 

It speaks of no work of the Law, but only that faith is to be placed in the cause of Christ.

Romans 10:9b-10 For if you confess with your mouth the Lord Jesus, and believe in your heart that God raised Him, you will be saved. For with the heart one believes unto righteousness, but with the mouth confession is made unto salvation. 

Whatever Paul said above, he has here made clear: this is the rule of faith, to believe that the Lord is Jesus, and not be ashamed to profess that God, raising Him from the dead, brought Him bodily into heaven, where He had come to be incarnate; lest one suffer the Gospel sentence which says: “Many, indeed, of the rulers believed in Jesus, but they did not openly profess it because of the Jews. For they loved the glory of men more than the glory of God” (Jn. 12:42).

Romans 10:11 For Scripture says through Isaiah: Everyone who believes in him shall not be put to shame (Isa. 28:16).

For when the examination of all things begins on the day of judgment, and all false notions or doctrines are brought to confusion, then those who believe in Christ will rejoice, seeing it revealed to all that what they believed is true, and what was thought foolish is wise. For among the others they will see themselves alone as glorious and wise, who had been considered disgraceful and foolish. For there is true approval there, where there is reward and condemnation.

Romans 10:12a For there is no distinction between Jew and Greek.

Paul says that all are generally either mistaken because of unbelief or exalted because of faith; for without Christ there is no salvation with God, but rather punishment or death. For neither will the privilege of the Fathers be able to praise the Jews; nor the giving of the Law, since they did not accept the merit and promise of the Fathers. The Gentiles, moreover, have no testimony by which they may be praised according to the flesh, unless they believe in Christ.

Romans 10:12b For the same Lord is Lord of all, rich toward all who call upon him. 

It reveals that this refers to everyone, whether Jews or Greeks; because without calling upon the Lord Christ no one lives with God. Since, then, he is Lord of all, as the apostle Peter agrees, saying: “This is,” he says, “the Lord of all”; (Acts 10:36) yet he is rich only toward those who call upon him, because they will receive the reward. But he is not rich toward the faithless, because they are not participants in his blessings; nor will they receive what they did not believe he would give. And yet he did not call God rich toward those who believe, but toward those who call upon him; so that, after believing, the soul may not cease asking for what it has always been taught by God to ask, as it is written in the Gospel according to Luke concerning the adversary, because he is subtle and crafty, that one must always pray: but to believers alone the forgiveness of sins is given. It follows, moreover, that one devoted to prayer is delivered from evil and can obtain what God has promised to those who watch with all their heart (Luke 18:1).

Romans 10:13 For everyone who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved. 

This was said in Micah1. For God himself, who appeared to Moses, says: My name is the Lord (Exod. 6:3). This is the Son of God, who was therefore called both Angel and God; so that he would not be thought to be the one from whom all things exist, but the one through whom all things exist, so that the fact that he was called God may refer to the unity of the Father and the Son: Angel, however, because he was sent by the Father as the messenger of the promised salvation. And he is said to have been sent so that he would not be believed to be the Father himself, but begotten by him. Therefore everyone who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved. Moses also spoke in this sense: Whoever, he says, will not listen to that prophet shall be exterminated from among the people (Deut. 18:18, 19). If he is the Lord of all, he is invoked by servants. And because it is true, he added:

1 This is stated in Joel. But although the cited passage is found in that prophet (Joel 2:32), it is preferable to present the author’s words with their blemishes than corrected without the authority of the copies.

Romans 10:14a How then shall they call upon him in whom they have not believed? 

Certainly the Jews themselves do not believe in Christ, whom he called Lord. This follows from what I said above, because one must first believe in order to have confidence in asking. Or how shall they believe him of whom they have not heard? It is clear that one cannot believe him to whom obedience is denied.

Romans 10:14b And how shall they hear without a preacher? 

Nor is this obscure, because whoever resists the preacher does not accept his authority.

Romans 10:15a Or how will they preach unless they are sent? 

Nor does this admit of question; for they will not be true apostles unless they are sent by Christ, nor will they be able to preach without authority; for no signs of virtues bear witness to them.

Romans 10:15b As it is written: How beautiful are the feet of those who proclaim peace, who proclaim good things! 

The prophet says this in Nahum (Nahum 1:15). By mentioning feet, he signifies the arrival of the apostles traveling throughout the world and proclaiming that the kingdom of God is approaching; for their coming enlightened people, showing the way by which one goes in peace to God, the way which John the Baptist had first come to prepare. This is the peace toward which those who believe in Christ hurry. Finally, holy Simeon, because there is discord in the world, rejoiced at the Savior’s birth: “Now, Lord,” he said, “let your servant depart in peace” (Luke 2:29); because the kingdom of God is peace. For when all discord has been removed, everyone will bend the knee to the one God. Finally, the heavenly Jerusalem is interpreted as the vision of peace, which is our mother.

Romans 10:16a But not all obey the Gospel. 

It is true, for although the world is illumined by the splendor of the Lord’s teaching, there are nevertheless those who resist, who call the light darkness. For their mind’s vision has been so dulled by error that they do not receive the radiance of the true light. The Gospel accuses them, saying: The light shines in the darkness, and the darkness did not comprehend it (Jn. 1:5).

Romans 10:16b For Isaiah says: Lord, who has believed our report (Isa. 53:1)?

That is, who has believed what we have heard from you and proclaim? Paul established by the prophetic example that the Jews are contradictors of the Gospel truth. For the Law accuses the Jews, because just as it was among the ancients, so it is now. For he does not belong to this number who does not receive the faith.

Romans 10:17 Therefore faith comes from hearing, and hearing through the word of Christ. 

It reveals that unless something is said, it can neither be heard nor believed. But I say: Have they not heard? That is, they heard, but did not wish to believe. For although faith comes from hearing, there are nevertheless those who do not believe what they hear. For they hear and do not understand, because their hearts have been blinded by hatred.

Romans 10:18 And indeed their sound has gone out into all the earth, and their words to the ends of the world. 

Paul testifies that the preaching of God was heard by the Jews to such an extent that he even declares the world filled with the divine announcement; for the very structure of the world proclaims the Creator. What the Psalmist said about creation (Ps. 18:5), the Apostle says here about the evangelists, because it was heard everywhere, and the preaching of Christ’s name reached every place. For where the presence of a preaching man was lacking, the sound and report nevertheless arrived, just as the report of the mighty deeds performed in Egypt had reached all nations, as Rahab the harlot testifies. For if it reached every place, the Jews could not have failed to hear the apostolic preaching, so that none of them might be excused from the crime of unbelief.

Romans 10:19a But I say: Did Israel not know? 

That is, did it not know? For through the testimonies mentioned above Paul accuses the people of Israel of unbelief, seeming sorrowfully to complain about all the Israelites: he does not deny that Israel knew and obtained what had been promised to it in the Law, but rather that Israel which is Israel in spirit more than in flesh, which God had also foreknown would believe. For all heard, but not all believed; therefore:

Romans 10:19b First Moses says, in the song of Deuteronomy: I will provoke you to jealousy by a non-nation, by a foolish nation I will anger you (Deut. 32:21).

These are the words of one angry, because the Jews were always found to be distrustful; and he assigns one generation and cause to all their evils. While he rebukes those present, he there especially touches upon those who were to come, to whom this applies. For all these share one condemnation if they persist in unbelief. Jealousy, therefore, arose in them from envy, when they saw a nation which, previously, because it was without God, was harsh in understanding, call its God the one who had been the God of the Jews, and attain the gift promised to them, through which, suffering jealousy, they would be stretched by anger, so that they might pay the due penalty for their wickedness and unbelief. For nothing consumes a person so much as jealousy, which God therefore appointed as the avenger of unbelief, because it is a grave sin. For, always sent into anger, they are tormented when they hear that the Law and the prophets belong to us who believe in Christ.

Romans 10:20 Isaiah dares and says: “I was revealed to those who did not seek me, and I was found among those who did not inquire of me” (Isa. 65:1).

Since above Paul used Moses’ words concerning the exclusion of the Jews, he also added this example from the prophet Isaiah, so that from its fuller context he might make clearer that God, having expelled the Jews, freely offered grace to the Gentiles, to the shame and destruction of the Jews. For the prophet Isaiah bears witness to this in the person of Christ.

Romans 10:21 But to Israel he says: “All day long I have stretched out my hands to a people who do not believe and who contradict” (Isa. 65:2).

Here the Israel meant is carnal, that is, the sons of Abraham, but not according to faith; for the true and spiritual Israel is the one who sees God by believing. “All day long” always signifies “continually”; for they were continually accused. Therefore they are rebuked for refusing to believe, so that they may know that they themselves are the cause of their destruction. This can also be understood of the Savior, who, with hands extended on the cross, condemned the sin of those killing him. For that passage cries out against the wicked deed of the Jews, which Isaiah above dared to say concerning those who had been enemies of God: he shows that they would become friends, and that those who were called Israelites, because they were disobedient, were to be rejected as enemies.

Chapter 11

Romans 11:1a I say therefore: Has God rejected His inheritance? By no means. 

Because Paul showed the people of Israel to be unbelieving, lest it be thought that he had called all Israel faithless, he shows that God has not rejected His inheritance, which He foreknew, that is, the sons of Abraham whom He knew would be faithful to Him. For He would not promise them the kingdom if He knew that none of them would believe. Therefore, according to the Law, the Lord’s inheritance is the sons of Abraham, but those who believe.

Romans 11:1b For I too am an Israelite, from the offspring of Abraham, of the tribe of Benjamin. 

By his own example Paul teaches that part of Israel has been saved, which God foreknew would be saved, or could still be saved; and he assigns part of Israel, because of its continual unbelief, to destruction.

Romans 11:2 God has not rejected His people whom He foreknew. 

This is what the Savior says: “Father, those whom You gave Me, I have guarded, and none of them has perished except the son of perdition” (Jn. 17:12). So too, of those whom God foreknew would believe, none has been rejected from the promise; for it happened just as God foreknew it would.

Romans 11:3-4 Do you not know what Scripture says in Elijah, how he pleads with God against Israel? “Lord, they have killed your prophets, they have dug up your altars: and I alone am left, and they seek my life” (1 Kings 19:10). But what does the divine response say to him? “I have kept for myself seven thousand men who have not bent the knee before Baal” (1 Kings 19:12).

 These things are clear; for Paul shows that not only Elijah remained, who, devoted to God, did not worship idols, but also many who remained in the faith of God, just as not a few of the Jews believed. History also agrees with this matter, because many were hidden in caves (1 Kings 18:4) on account of Ahab, king of Samaria, and his wife Jezebel, who, believing the false prophets, persecuted God’s prophets and urged the people toward idolatry.

Romans 11:5 Therefore, also at this present time, a remnant has been saved according to the election of grace. 

That is, Paul says, even now these have remained in the promise of the Law, while many whom God foreknew have departed. For those who received Christ as promised in the Law stood in the Law; but those who did not receive him departed from the Law. Therefore believers are called the remnant, that is, those remaining in the Law.

Romans 11:6a But if by grace, then no longer of works. 

It is evident that grace is the gift of God, not a due reward for works, but granted freely, with mercy intervening.

Romans 11:6b Otherwise grace is no longer grace. 

This is true, because if it is a reward, it is not grace; but since it is not a reward, undoubtedly it is grace, because to grant pardon to sinners is nothing other than grace, and it is offered to those who do not seek it, so that they may believe. Grace, therefore, is twofold, because this befits God, who abounds in mercy: both to seek those whom He may freely heal.

Romans 11:7 What then? What Israel was seeking, it did not attain; but the elect attained it? 

The Israelites are those who are carnal, who, thinking that they could be justified by works of the Law, did not attain to being righteous through faith before God. Therefore, since all are guilty through the Law, for “cursed is everyone who does not continue in all the things written in the book of the Law, to do them” (Deut. 27:26), those who believed themselves to be justified through faith attained election, so that, having been justified, they might become sons of God, mindful of the Scripture that says: “For the righteous shall live by faith” (Hab. 2:4), that is, not by the Law.

Romans 11:8-10 But the others were blinded, as it is written; this passage is read in Isaiah: God gave them a spirit of remorse, eyes so that they would not see, and ears so that they would not hear, to this very day (Isa. 6:9). And David says: Let their table become a snare, and a trap, and a stumbling block, and a recompense for them (Ps. 68:23). Let their eyes be darkened, so that they may not see, and always bend their backs.

Therefore he curses the table of the wicked, because there the innocent are accustomed to be deceived; for they are deceitfully invited to a banquet so that they may be destroyed. Therefore Ammon, David’s son, was deceived by his brother Absalom (2 Kings 13:27), and the wicked Holofernes thought he could deceive the holy Judith at a feast (Judith 12:1), and at the table of the criminal Herod the wicked rejoiced over the head of John the prophet (Mark 6:8). The two prophets have one meaning concerning such people, who from the beginning of faith were found to be resisting enemies of the good.
