But some of them were corrected and revised, though late; others, however, remaining in stubbornness, did not escape the severity of the sentence, being forever bent, that is, blinded; so that thereafter they could not look upon the way of truth (which they had scornfully rejected through hatred, turning away from it) and attain the grace of salvation. For whoever, after understanding, refused to believe ought to obtain what he desires: that thereafter he be unable to receive faith and be saved. The examples of the prophets mentioned above therefore contain a twofold meaning; for they were decreed and written in two ways. Indeed, two classes of people are contained here. One is blinded forever because of its hatred, so that it may not be saved; for they are of the worst will, to such an extent that, while understanding, they say they do not know what they hear. Finally, they said of the Savior: What does he mean? We do not know what he is saying. Why do you listen to him? He has a demon and is insane (Jn. 10:20). But the other group, which is truly Israel, contradicted them: These words do not come from one who has a demon. Can a demon open the eyes of the blind? While they envied the Savior, therefore, they did not wish to appear to understand what they heard; so that, since he seemed not to be understood by the lawyers and Pharisees, he would be thought to speak absurd things and things outside the law—something that could also turn others away from the faith. Hence they hear from the Savior: You have taken away the key of knowledge; you yourselves do not enter, and you prevent others from entering (Luke 11:52). For who would not follow the counsel and judgment of the lawyers and Pharisees—not of the frivolous, but of the somewhat more serious—who appeared to be defenders of the Law? Finally, they said to those who were truly Israelites: Has any of the rulers believed in him? so that they might rightly seem not to have believed, if they had made many accomplices in their conspiracy; because what is defended by few usually does not appear approved. Therefore they were blinded, so that thereafter they could not believe and be saved. Thus they were helped according to their own will; since, knowing the truth, they called it false, they would thereafter not understand what is true, so that they might hold as true the falsehood they desired. The other class is that which, while pursuing the righteousness of the Law, does not receive the righteousness of Christ: hence it is no longer acting through the envy of an evil will, but is blinded for a time through the error of zealously imitating the tradition of the Fathers; for they ought to learn from Christ’s mighty deeds that he cannot deceive, whose magnificent power is evident in his works, and compare his preaching with the new testament promised through the prophets, and then profess that he is the one who was promised. They were found to be neglectful toward God and to side with men. Therefore they are blinded so that, by joining themselves to their promise through that very zeal, while they envy the Gentiles, they may return to faith in God.

Romans 11:11a I say therefore: Have they stumbled so as to fall? By no means. 

Paul says what I mentioned above: they did not so lose faith that they would never believe again; that is, they were not blinded by their wickedness to such an extent that they could not be healed, just as we read that the Devil fell, when the prophet Isaiah says: “How Lucifer fell from heaven” (Isa. 14:12), signifying a fall and apostasy. Therefore, he does not say that they stumbled so as to fall, but that, because of their offense, they were temporarily hardened.

Romans 11:11b But through their trespass salvation came to the Gentiles, so that they might rival them. 

This is what Paul said: salvation was given to the Gentiles because of their sin. For because the Jews rejected God’s gift, it was transferred to the Gentiles, so that the Jews, kindled by the zeal of rivalry, might be converted to Christ, jealous for the promise made to the fathers.

Romans 11:12 But if their trespass is the riches of the world, and their shrinking is the riches of the Gentiles, how much more their fullness! 

It reveals that if their trespass benefited the world, since through their loss it has more good people, for the Gentiles are far more numerous than the Jews, and their shrinking, which is the loss of the promise, is the riches of the Gentiles, through which they have gained eternal life, how much more their fullness! It is clear that the world will be richer in good people if those who have been blinded are also converted; for the world will be saved in large part. “The world” means people, as it was said of the Savior: “Behold, the whole world has gone after him” (Jn. 12:19).

Romans 11:13-14 For I speak to you Gentiles: Inasmuch as I am the apostle of the Gentiles, I honor my ministry, if somehow I may rival my own flesh and save some of them.

Paul shows the Gentiles how warmly he loves the Jews: for he honors his ministry, by which he is the apostle of the Gentiles, if, because of his affection for his own race, he also works to bring the Jews to the faith. For he will become more honored if he wins to life even those to whom he was not sent: for it will be the greatest praise for the Fathers if he finds brothers who had perished.

Romans 11:15 For if their loss is the reconciliation of the world, what will their acceptance be but life from the dead? 

Paul says this because, if the Gentiles were reconciled to God through the unbelief of the Jews, for the world’s increase in faith in Christ, what will be the extent and fullness of salvation in this matter if the Jews are brought to faith in Christ, except that the world may be brought to life from the dead in its people? Therefore, since there has been progress, he says that efforts should be made so that they may believe; for the dullness of blindness is removed when the appointed time of sin has been completed, so that they may receive the free choice of their own will.

Romans 11:16 And if the first fruits are holy, so is the lump. 

It is evident that what is of one substance is one; and therefore the first fruits cannot be holy while the lump is unclean, for the first fruits are from the lump. Therefore Paul shows that those whose fathers have already obtained faith cannot be called unworthy of faith; for if part of the Jews believed, why should it not also be said that the other part can believe?

Romans 11:17 And if the root is holy, so are the branches. 

Repeating the same thought with different words, Paul praises the meaning with two examples. But if some of the branches were broken off, that is, if some of them did not believe, cut off from the promise, you, although you were a wild olive shoot, were grafted among them and became a sharer in the root and richness of the olive tree. This signifies that, because many Jews did not believe, the Gentiles were grafted in through faith into the hope of the promise, causing grief to the Jews, but not according to the law of agriculture, since they graft a good shoot onto a bad tree; for these, though they were in a bad root, were grafted onto a good tree. Therefore he says that the wild olive was grafted in, so that the shoot might bear the fruit of the root, having become its partner.

Romans 11:18a Do not boast against the branches, 

That is, do not rejoice in their unbelief; for it displeases God if anyone exults in another’s misfortunes, as Solomon says (Prov. 24:17). For they were not cast off for the sake of the Gentiles, so that they might rejoice; but because they did not believe, they gave occasion for the Gentiles to be preached to.

Romans 11:18b But if you boast, you do not bear the root; rather, the root bears you. 

That is, if you exalt yourself over those into whose root you have been grafted, you insult the race that received you so that you might be good from what was evil; nor will you stand if you destroy that by which you stand.

Romans 11:19 You say, then: The branches were broken off so that I might be grafted in. 

Paul speaks in the person of a believing Gentile, because he thinks one should rejoice at the downfall of the unbelieving Jews, saying: Their rejection made room for the Gentiles. But they were not rejected by God so that the Gentiles might enter; rather, they made themselves rejected by despising God’s gift, and therefore gave the Gentiles an opportunity for salvation. He wishes to suppress this arrogance, so that one may rejoice more in salvation and not insult another’s suffering; for the one who rejoices in others’ misfortunes is easily deceived.

Romans 11:20a Well, that is, you speak correctly, because you have been grafted in among the broken branches; but they were broken off because of unbelief,

That is, not because of you, but through their own fault; for when they were unbelieving, you were called to salvation to provoke them to jealousy. Therefore you ought to give thanks for God’s gift through Christ, not insult them; but also pray that, if their downfall has brought you salvation, they too may return to their origin. Then you will please God, who has shown you mercy; for he called you for this reason: that through your jealousy he might restore them also to grace.

Romans 11:20b But you stand by faith. 

Because the Jews fell through unbelief, Paul says that these stand by faith; for although they previously lay fallen because of unfaithfulness, they began to stand by believing. Do not be arrogant, but fear. That is, do not be proud, but beware lest you also fall.

Romans 11:21 For if God did not spare the natural branches, perhaps He will not spare you either. 

It is true that if He blinded those who were worthy by the privilege of the Fathers, to whom the promise had also been made that they would be adopted as God’s children, because of unbelief, what will He do to those who doubt or exalt themselves, who have been elevated by no recommendation, for though they had no dignity, they were honored?

Romans 11:22 See, therefore, the goodness and severity of God: toward those who fell, severity; but toward you, goodness, if you continue in goodness; otherwise you too will be cut off. 

Paul testifies that God is good to the Gentiles, because, although they followed idols and were certainly deserving of death, in His patience He waited for them, and, even while they did not yet seek Him, He freely called them and forgave their sins. But toward the Jews He is severe; for He blinded them because they rejected the gift of God. Here, however, he now refers to those Jews who, because of their hatred, have been blinded forever; therefore he also says that they have fallen. But those whom he mentioned above have stumbled, yet have not fallen, because he shows that they were blinded for a time; and therefore God was severe toward those apostates, so that they would be blinded forever.

Romans 11:23 But if they do not persist in unbelief, they too will be grafted in, for God is able to graft them in again. 

Paul demonstrates that God’s justice does not consist in severity toward those whom He blinded for a time, because He did not cut them off in such a way that He could not graft them in again if they repent, He who said through the prophet: “I will replant those who return to me” (Jer. 24:6), so that Christian Gentiles, knowing this, may not insult the Jews, certain that God’s mercy is reserved for them as for those who have fallen.

Romans 11:24 For if you were cut from the wild olive tree by nature, and contrary to nature were grafted into the good olive tree, how much more will those who are according to nature be grafted into their own olive tree! 

Let us understand the olive tree as the faith by which Abraham was justified; but the wild olive tree, because its nature is wild and unfruitful, signifies faithlessness: and therefore, if those who were always enemies of God, converted and grafted into the faith of Abraham, are grafted in, although they are not descended from him, how much more will the Jews, if they believe after their disbelief, be restored to their ancestral nature and grafted again into their own promise!

Romans 11:25-26a For I do not want you to be ignorant of this mystery, brothers, lest you be wise in your own estimation: a partial hardening has come upon Israel, until the fullness of the Gentiles enters; and therefore all Israel will be saved.

It is not obscure: the Jews were given a temporary blindness, those who, while zealous for the Law, did not see that the gift promised by God had come, which was preached by Christ. For they were blinded by zeal, thinking that the law of works should never cease; therefore they were zealous for the Sabbath. Because of this offense they were hardened in part, so that they might suffer for their unbelief, seeing the Gentiles joyfully professing that they had obtained the promise made to Abraham. Yet, when the abundance of the Gentiles had been admitted, the darkness would be wiped from the eyes of their minds, so that they could believe; and the spirit of remorse, which prevents their hearts and grants them blindness, would restore to them the free choice of the will; because their unbelief did not arise from hatred but from error, so that they might be corrected and afterward saved.

Romans 11:26b As it is written. 

To prove that the gift reserved for them by God, he sets forth below the example of the prophet Isaiah, so that he might teach that by this grace, through which believing Jews have been freed, we too can be freed; for it had not been exhausted, but always abounds. Therefore he says:

Romans 11:27 He will come from Zion who redeems and turns away wickedness from Jacob; and this is my covenant with them, when I take away their sins (Isa. 59:28).

The meaning of this example always remains, as long as there are those who believe. For this is the Lord Jesus, who was promised to come from heaven for the liberation of the human race; he himself daily forgives the sins of those who turn to him. Nor does he immediately condemn unbelievers, but waits, knowing that they may advance toward the knowledge of God.

Romans 11:28a According to the Gospel, they are indeed enemies for your sake. 

The cause of their unbelief is that they are enemies of the Gospel, so that their error and transgression might open the way for the nations, before the appointed time, to enter the faith, as I mentioned above; for first the Gospel had to be preached everywhere to all the Jews, and only then was it necessary for the nations also to believe the word of God. But because they did not believe, the kingdom was taken from them and given to the nations. Therefore he warns that they should not be insulted, whose transgression benefited the nations; rather, the one should be insulted whose sin harmed others. For one should not rejoice in their unfaithfulness, but grieve if they delay in converting; so that, just as the nations rejoiced at their sin because they were saved, so they may rejoice at their conversion, for through their occasion they received God’s grace more quickly.

Romans 11:28b But according to election, they are beloved for the sake of the Fathers. 

Although the Jews sinned gravely by rejecting God’s gift and are worthy of death, nevertheless, because they are children of good men, through whose privilege and merit they received many benefits from God, they will be received with joy when they return to the faith; for in them God’s love is stirred up by the memory of the Fathers.

Romans 11:29 For the gifts and calling of God are binding. 

It is true, because God’s grace in baptism does not seek groaning, or weeping, or any work, but only a profession from the heart. Nor, because they had grievously sinned by not accepting God’s promise, and because grave sins get forgiveness only through weeping and groaning, did he show that they could no longer receive mercy because he did not see them grieving; he shows that this is not required at the beginning of faith, for God’s gift freely forgives sins in baptism.

Romans 11:30-31 For just as you once did not believe God, but now have obtained mercy because of their unbelief, so they too now have not believed in your mercy, so that they themselves may also obtain mercy. 

Paul recalls the unbelief of the Gentiles, so that they would not boastfully insult the Jews who had not believed, but would take pleasure in recognizing God’s promise. For, he says, you Gentiles, although the oracles of God had been entrusted to you with them, were rebellious; but now you have received mercy, not through your own merit, but to their blame. Why, then, should these not receive mercy when converted, who previously lived according to God’s law and to whom the promise was made?

Romans 11:32 For God has entrusted all to disobedience, that he may have mercy on all. 

Moreover, the Gentiles from ancient times lived in wickedness and ignorance, as those without God; therefore the Law was revealed in writing, by which they might be restrained from rushing headlong. And since, through the adversary’s cunning, sins began to multiply, so that by the prohibition man might be made more guilty, God, in the mercy of his goodness, always providing for man, so that what was sin without the Law might also be erased under the Law, decreed this: that he should establish faith alone, through which the sins of all might be abolished; so that, since there was no hope for all people through the Law, they might be saved by God’s mercy. This is what it means to have entrusted all to disobedience: that then the decreed gift might come from God, when all were afflicted by disbelief, so that the grace of the gift might be most welcome. Therefore let no one boast; for it is wretched to be proud when one has been forgiven.

Romans 11:33 O the depth of the riches, wisdom, and knowledge of God! How unsearchable are his judgments, and how mysterious his ways! 

Paul bears witness to the exalted and boundless God, in the riches of his wisdom and knowledge, with all praise and thanksgiving; for his plan and judgment are incomprehensible. For, knowing from the beginning the conduct and deeds of human beings, since the human race could be saved neither by justice alone in its severity, nor by mercy alone attain the advancement of merits, he decreed at each time what should be preached, while nevertheless allowing each person to follow his own judgment; for justice is known by nature itself as its guide. And because the authority of natural justice had grown dull through the habit of sinning, the Law was given, so that the human race might be restrained by fear of the Law revealed. But because those who did not restrain themselves were held guilty by the Law, mercy was proclaimed, which would save those fleeing to it, but would blind those rejecting it for a time, inviting the nations to their promised calling, those who previously refused to follow the justice of God given through Moses, so that, while envying their salvation, they might through that very zeal be converted to the origin of the Savior from the root promised in the Law. This is the depth of the riches, wisdom, and knowledge of God, who by many providence acquired both Jews and Gentiles for life.

Romans 11:34-35 For who has known the mind of the Lord? Or who has been His counselor? Or who first gave to Him, and it shall be repaid to him (Isa. 40:13)?

This is written in Isaiah. It is clear that God alone knows all counsels, and that He alone needs nothing; for all things are from Him. Therefore, His counsel is neither comprehended nor measured by anyone, because those below cannot know the mind of Him who is above. Finally, to the believing Jews it seemed impossible that God’s counsel and will could be for the redemption of the Gentiles; likewise, it seemed difficult and unbelievable to the Gentiles that the Jews who had not believed could be converted, or that believers could be received so that they might be saved. Among other things, this is the counsel of God that remained hidden and could not be comprehended.

Romans 11:36 For from him, and through him, and in him are all things: to him be glory. 

Paul declared that the meaning and plan of God cannot be investigated: “For from him,” he says, “and through him, and in him are all things: to him be glory.” By this statement he revealed a meaning that was hidden from the world; for since God is the creator of all things (for he made things that did not exist, so that they might exist), therefore all things are from him. And since they are from him, through his Son, who is certainly of the same substance, they began to exist, whose work is the Father’s work. Therefore, since he works through the Son, all things are through him. And since the things that are from God are through God: after being reborn in the Holy Spirit, all things are in him, because the Holy Spirit too is from God the Father; therefore he also knows what is in God. Therefore the Father is also in the Holy Spirit; because that which is from God the Father cannot be other than what God the Father is.

And therefore to him be glory, because from him, and through him, and in him are all things. Therefore those things which began to exist from him, through him, and in him cannot know his meaning and plan. But he knows all things, because all things are in him. He disclosed the mystery of God, which he previously said should not be unknown to them.

Chapter 12

Romans 12:1–2 I beg you therefore, brethren, by the mercy of God, that you present your bodies as a living, holy sacrifice, pleasing to God, your reasonable service. And do not conform yourselves to this age, but be transformed by the renewal of your mind, so that you may prove what is the will of God, what is good, pleasing, and perfect.

Paul urges them by the mercy of God through which the human race is saved. After treating of the Law and the faith of the Jewish and Gentile peoples, he urges them to lead a good life; for through this the hope of faith is obtained. This warning is therefore given, that they may remember that they have received mercy, and be watchful concerning the service of Him from whom it was given to them, through whom they were freely justified from ungodliness, knowing that this is a living, holy, and acceptable sacrifice to God, if we keep our bodies undefiled, unlike worldly people who pursue pleasures. This is the will of God, your sanctification (1 Thess. 4:3). For bodies subjected to sins are not considered living, but dead; for they are under the despair of the promised life. For this purpose we are washed from sins by God’s gift, so that from this time, leading a pure life, we may stir up God’s love toward us, not making the work of His grace void. For among the ancients the offering was killed for this reason: to signify that people, subjected to death because of sin, were represented by it. But now, because people have been purified by God’s gift and freed from the second death, they ought to offer a living sacrifice, so that it may be a sign of eternal life. For, as then, bodies were not sacrificed for bodies; now, however, not bodies but the vices of the body must be put to death. But in order that we may make our service effective, while preserving justice, let us guard modesty; for modesty accomplishes nothing, nor can it be considered reasonable, if justice is neglected. This is to be conformed to spiritual things, renewed in spirit and faith, to know what pleases God, and that nothing else is good and perfect.

Romans 12:3 For I say, through the grace given to me, to everyone among you, not to think more highly than he ought to think, but to think soberly, and to each according as God has apportioned the measure of faith. 

Paul clearly shows that we ought to think in such a way as not to exceed the bounds of righteousness, so that it may be useful not only to ourselves but harm no one. For this is carefulness: to benefit some rather than harm them; and one ought to be content with the lot that God has measured out, and with the merit of each person’s faith; nor should anyone claim for himself what he sees has not been granted to him. This is, not to think more highly, because the whole cannot be granted to one person. For even if someone leads a good life, he should not therefore claim for himself the wisdom of teaching; nor, because he possesses knowledge of the Law, should he claim the services of the Levites. Therefore he exhorts and teaches through the grace given to him. This grace is understood as knowledge of the Lord’s teaching, through which he instructs that humility and righteousness must be pursued.

Romans 12:4-5 For just as in one body we have many members, but all the members do not have the same function, so we, though many, are one body in Christ, and individually members of one another.

By the example of the body Paul teaches that none of us can do everything alone, because we are members of one another, so that one does not lack the other; and therefore we ought to care for one another, and no one should hinder another, because we need one another’s services. This will be loving Christ: if the members exhort one another to fulfill the role by which the body may be perfected in Christ.

Romans 12:6a Having different gifts of God according to the grace that has been given to us. 

Now Paul reckons the offices assigned to the members according to the merit of faith, so that when a member sees what office has been assigned to it, it may not complain against another, to which it sees something else entrusted, but may rejoice with it, so that the body of the Church may be perfect.

Romans 12:6b Or prophecy according to the rule of faith. 

Paul begins with prophecy, which is the first proof that our faith is rational. Indeed, those who believed, having received the Spirit, prophesied. This, therefore, is given according to the measure of the recipient, that is, as much as the circumstance requires for which it is given.

Romans 12:7a Whether ministry in serving. 

A minister is strengthened to provide the service of brotherhood to the extent that he believes he ought to serve; lest, laboring beyond faith in service, he be wearied in vain; for each person receives what he strives for from the heart.

Romans 12:7b Or one who teaches in instruction. 

Similarly, Paul says that the teacher is helped in teaching, so that to the extent his faith is directed toward teaching, he may be inspired to impart heavenly discipline.

Romans 12:8a Whether one encourages, in encouragement. 

Such a person is likewise prepared by the help of the Spirit in the very task toward which he strives, so that he may have grace while he urges others on. For he challenges the brethren to goodness, or unbelievers to faith.

Romans 12:8b He who gives, in simplicity. 

To this person Paul says that one who gives assistance with a good disposition is always supplied with aid, the Spirit providing it, so that the one who gives simply may not lack, as Solomon says: “He who gives to the poor shall not lack” (Prov. 28:27). He therefore gives simply who does not do this artificially, so that praise may be given him by men, but so that he may obtain merit for this very thing from God.

Romans 12:8c He who oversees, with carefulness. 

Whoever undertakes the care of overseeing over the brethren is to receive, according to his faith, care and authority, so that he may prosper in that for which he is careful, having fruit in those over whom he leads.

Romans 12:8d He who shows mercy, with cheerfulness. 

This person also, according to his disposition, who shows mercy with a joyful spirit, not as though it were extorted from one unwilling, is founded and strengthened by God, so that he may suffer no weakness in this matter, knowing what Solomon said: “While you have the opportunity, do good” (Prov. 3:27). But this is to be understood in many ways; for mercy is diverse under one name. For if above Paul said, “He who gives, in simplicity,” what need was there to repeat it, unless by the one name of mercy he signified many good works? For to forgive sinners is mercy; to bring help to one placed in some need or oppressed is mercy; to clothe the naked, to break bread for the hungry, to take in an abandoned child, to cover a corpse, and to perform other such works is mercy. Therefore, if he performs these things diligently and without weariness, he will have the reward of this work both in the present and in the future. Therefore every act of mercy ought to be done both simply and with joy, so that simplicity excludes hypocrisy, while joy bears witness to confidence in the hope of future reward.

Romans 12:9a Love without deception. 

The Spirit aids this mind, which reflects that it should love its brother because it knows this pleases God the Creator; so that it does not do this in flattery of the present life, and so that, because it seeks this with a devoted mind, it may fulfill it through acts of service. For they hear the Lord say: “I give you a new commandment, that you love one another” (Jn. 13:34).

Romans 12:9b-10 Hating evil, observing to what is good, being kindly affectionate to one another with brotherly love, anticipating one another in mutual honor. 

Paul does not consider it a great thing to avoid evil unless one follows to what is good; for the former arises from fear, the latter from love. Nor is one said to be kind or to maintain brotherly love if they have not anticipated one another in mutual acts of service.

Romans 12:11a Not sluggish in carefulness. 

This is what the prophet Jeremiah says (Jer. 48:10): “Cursed is he who does the work of the Lord negligently.” For one who is sluggish in divine conduct is without hope; therefore Paul added:

Romans 12:11b Fervent in spirit. 

That is, in the practice of divine work or of the Law, let him not be lukewarm, as it is said in the Apocalypse of John: “Because you are lukewarm,” he says, “I will vomit you out of my mouth” (Rev. 3:16). Daily meditation removes sluggishness and makes one vigilant. The works of the Lord are those Paul commands to be done for the benefit of the Church, that is, of the brethren.

Romans 12:11c Serving the time. 

In Greek it is said therefore: serving God; but this does not fit the passage itself. For what need was there to place here this summary of all devotion, when Paul mentions the individual members that pertain to obedience and service to God? For in all those things he lists, full service to the Lord is rendered. Elsewhere he explains what it means to serve the time, when he says: “Redeeming the time, because the days are evil; so that you may know how to answer each one” (Ephes. 5:16). Since he had said, “Fervent in spirit,” lest they understand this as meaning that they should indiscriminately and inopportunely thrust religious words upon hostile times, thereby perhaps causing scandal, he immediately added: “Serving the time,” so that they might speak the faith of religion modestly and honorably, at suitable times, in suitable places, and to suitable persons. For there are some even in this time of peace who so hate the words of God that, when they hear them, they blaspheme the way of Christ with great anger. Paul himself also served the time when he did what he did not wish; for unwillingly he circumcised Timothy, and, having shaved his head, went up to the temple to be purified according to the Law, so as to calm the madness of the Jews.

Romans 12:12a Rejoicing in hope. 

After saying, “Serving the time,” Paul added, “Rejoicing in hope”; so that if, perhaps, because of the wickedness of the time, it is not permitted to speak publicly about the faith, but one must live in fear, let him rejoice in hope; because this sadness produces joy.

Romans 12:12b Patient in tribulation. 

This is to rejoice in hope: to be patient in tribulation; for because of the joy of hope, he endures tribulation, knowing that the things promised in return for these are much greater.

Romans 12:12c Constant in prayer. 

Prayer is very necessary; because, in order that tribulation may be endured, one must persist in prayers.

Romans 12:13 Sharing in the memories of the saints

It is clear that whoever wishes his prayers to be heard must strive to imitate the lives of the saints; for when he imitates them, he shares in them. Therefore, to remember and share in them means to imitate their deeds; and if they are in need of material support, it must be shared with them, as Paul says elsewhere: “Concerning the collections made for the saints” (1 Cor. 16:1); and to the Galatians: “That we should remember the poor” (Gal. 2:10).

Romans 12:14a Pursuing hospitality. 

The imitator and lover of the saints pursues hospitality, following the examples of Saint Abraham and Lot, the righteous man.

Romans 12:14b Bless those who persecute you; bless, and do not curse. 

Paul wishes to make Christians altogether new, and therefore also wants to remove from them this habit, which is common to all: lest, provoked by an angry spirit, they readily wish first to curse; but rather, having conquered their anger, let them bless, and let the Lord’s discipline be praised.

Romans 12:15 To rejoice with those who rejoice, to weep with those who weep. 

This is what Paul says elsewhere: If one member suffers, all the members suffer with it; and if one member is honored, all the members rejoice with it (1 Cor. 12:26). For when someone offers faithful support in distress, he lifts up the person’s spirit and gains merit for himself before God, because he loves a member of Christ’s body; and if he grieves with an unbeliever, he spurs that person all the more toward progress in the Lord’s discipline.

Romans 12:16a Being mutually sympathetic. 

That is, one ought to share in a brother’s trouble, as Paul also says in another epistle: “Considering yourself, lest you also be tempted. Bear one another’s burdens” (Gal. 6:1-2).

Romans 12:16b Not being wise in high matters. 

To be wise in high matters is pride; for even the Devil, when he aspired to high things, fell away. If there is exaltation in the mind, and someone, presuming perhaps on the soundness of his conduct, does not grieve with his brother but scolds him as though he were a sinner, this is pride, which, by putting himself first, gives offense. The Lord also points this out, saying: “First cast the beam out of your own eye, and then you will see to cast the splinter out of your brother’s eye” (Luke 6:42). For exaltation itself is sin; and even if he were not a sinner, which is impossible, he becomes a sinner by being proud. Finally Solomon says: “God resists the proud” (Prov. 3:34).

Romans 12:16c But accepting with the humble. 

That is, having laid aside pride, let him make another’s cause his own, and his own as another’s, so that he may have grace with God, because “whoever exalts himself will be humbled” (Luke 14:11).

Romans 12:16d Do not be wise in your own eyes (Isa. 5:21).

This is written in the prophet Isaiah, which Paul here sets down as his own, so that justice may be held in common, and not that one person be just to himself but unjust to everyone else.

Romans 12:17a Returning evil for evil to no one. 

This is what the Lord says: Unless your righteousness exceeds that of the scribes and Pharisees, you will not enter the kingdom of God (Matt. 5:20). For the commandment in the Law was: You shall love your neighbor, and hate your enemy (Lev. 19:18). This, as it seems, is justice; but so that the righteousness of Christians may abound, they are taught not to return evil for evil, so that they may be perfect and receive a reward for this at God’s judgment. For he seems to go beyond justice itself who, in order to be better, does not do what is permitted, as an imitator of heavenly justice. The justice of the world, therefore, granted to him by God makes him innocent; but heavenly justice makes him perfect, so that he may have merit before God.

Romans 12:17b Providing what is good not only before God, but also before all people. 

To provide means to keep future good things before one’s eyes, so that deeds may be performed which, once done, cannot be subject to blame, but rather may be praiseworthy, whether before God or before people. Let no one think that, because things permitted by God are not displeasing to Him, one need not care if they are a stumbling block to a brother; this passage warns that what is done must both not displease God and not be a stumbling block to a brother. For even if something is permitted, if it causes a brother to stumble, it does not please God, because God commands us to care for his salvation. Therefore good is provided before God and people if permitted things are done in such a way that they cause no stumbling.

Romans 12:18 If possible, so far as it depends on you, live at peace with all people. 

Everyone who preserves divine justice wishes to be peaceable. Let anyone see to it if he despises this peace, so as to become an enemy to such a man, either because he does not wish to be criticized by him, or because he envies his good qualities; yet, as far as it concerns him, he does not disagree with those who act rightly. For this is the unpeaceable man who, abandoning the will of the Law, follows his own right, although David says: “With those who hated peace, I was peaceable” (Ps. 119:7). But this was necessary because of the power of the persons involved, so that by acts of humility he might overcome one whom pride so exalts that he despises the commandments of the Law. It may also happen that, out of fear of God, someone shows himself peaceable toward one who hates peace. For when he does not wish to repay evil with evil, he is peaceable, that is, he overcomes evil with good, so that by acts of obedience he may overcome one whom the commandments of the Law do not overcome. Therefore, Paul says, “if possible, so far as it depends on you,” so that we may be seen to have peace while doing good. Now, if he is not a lover of peace, you nevertheless wish to be peaceable, insofar as it concerns you. But if someone is profane and blasphemous, and you cannot have peace with him, it certainly will not be attributed to you; for the apostle John did not even permit those who deny that Christ came in the flesh to be greeted (2 Jn. 7). We, then, are ready, if possible, to live at peace with all; but when others resist, it becomes impossible, not because of us, provided no complaint is brought against us. For whoever harms no one plainly appears to be peaceable.

Romans 12:19a Do not defend yourselves, beloved, but give place to wrath. 

Paul advises that anger be set aside so that the bonds of peace may be preserved, especially since anger commonly leads to sin, when someone, stirred by anger, demands more than the cause of the offense warrants; or brings harm upon himself if he seeks to punish graver sins with an unsuitable vengeance, for he makes weak one whom he could have kept corrected and sound. Therefore Solomon also says: “Do not be excessively righteous”; for, he says, “there is one who perishes in his own righteousness” (Eccles. 7:17). For wishing to answer each sin, he may find death in the course of vengeance, both for himself and for the one whom he afflicts with blows; for people often commit offenses in punishing. He forbids suitable vengeance not only against subordinates, but also against equals and superiors; that is, if perhaps a brother sins against us, let us not seek revenge, but forgive, leaving it to God’s judgment, lest, while we are held by anger, the enemy find an opportunity to suggest and persuade us of what is against us.

Romans 12:19b For it is written. 

To strengthen his exhortation, Paul confirms this by the example of the Law, saying: For it is written in Proverbs,

Romans 12:19c “Vengeance is mine, and I will repay, says the Lord” (Deut. 32:35).

Therefore, if we fail entirely to do what he teaches, he shows that God is despised by us. Therefore, if vengeance is entrusted to God, we benefit in two ways: for when he conquers anger, he becomes perfect, and he will be avenged by God’s judgment.

Romans 12:20 If your enemy is hungry, feed him; if he is thirsty, give him drink; for by doing this, you will heap burning coals upon his head (Prov. 25:21).

Paul shows that not mere vengeance should be reserved for God, but that benefits should also be given to enemies, so that we may truly show that we have enemies not because of our merits, and that we strive to overcome them with kindness so that they may cease from their enmity. If they persist in hostility through the wickedness of their mind, our kindnesses will benefit them unto punishment; or certainly, pierced by the diligence of our kindnesses, they will revive like dead coals. Therefore, in order to make us so perfect that we may acquire not only ourselves but also others for life, the Lord, through Solomon, not only forbids us to repay our enemies in kind, but also urges us to invite them to friendship through compassion.

Romans 12:21 Do not be overcome by evil, but overcome evil with good. 

This is the Apostle’s interpretation, encouraging us not to repay our enemies in kind, as has been said. For it will greatly benefit us if we yield to hatred; indeed, evil conquers the one who seems for a time to be overcome by it. For the Savior likewise conquered evil by not resisting; hatred turns against itself, and while being defeated, thinks it is victorious. For the enemy’s aim is to draw us away from our purpose as well, seeking an opportunity for us to sin. Therefore, if provoked by him, we do not repay him in kind, we conquer him with good; for this reason we do not resist, so that we may preserve goodness, setting justice aside, because justice compels us to retaliate.

Chapter 13

Romans 13:1a Be subject to all higher authorities; for there is no authority except from God. 

Since Paul commanded that the law of heavenly justice be followed, lest it seem that he disregarded the present one, he praises this one: for unless this is observed, that cannot be kept; indeed, this is like a tutor who instructs children so that they may follow the path of the higher justice. For mercy cannot be attributed to anyone unless he possesses justice. Therefore, to confirm the right and fear of the natural law, he testifies that God is its author, and that those administering it possess God’s ordinance. Therefore he added:

Romans 13:1b And those that exist have been ordained by God. 

So that no one may think them shameful as mere human inventions; for they see that divine right has been assigned to human authorities. Therefore, he is subject to authority who, out of fear of God, abstains from those things it forbids.

Romans 13:2 Therefore whoever resists the authority resists God’s ordinance. 

This is directed against those who are powerful through their influence, or who do not believe that they can be caught by anyone, and therefore think they can mock the law; it shows them that the law belongs to God, and that those who escape judgment for a time by some means will not evade God’s judgment. But those who resist acquire condemnation for themselves. It is clear that everyone will either be justified or condemned by their own works. For those who sin while hearing the Law are without excuse.

Romans 13:3a For those who rule are not a terror to good conduct, but to evil. 

Paul calls these rulers kings, who are created to correct life and prevent wrongdoing, bearing the image of God, so that others may be subject to one.

Romans 13:3b Do you wish not to fear authority? Do what is good, and you will receive praise from it. 

Praise from authority arises when someone is found innocent.

Romans 13:4a For he is God’s minister to you for good. 

It is clear that rulers have been appointed so that evil may not be done.

Romans 13:4b But if you do evil, be afraid; for he does not carry the sword without cause. 

That is, he threatens for this reason: so that, if he is despised, he may punish.

Romans 13:4c For he is God’s minister, an avenger for wrath against the one who does evil. 

Since God has appointed a future judgment and wills that no one perish, he has ordained rulers in this age, so that, by instilling fear, they may be like instructors to all, teaching them what they should observe, lest they fall under the punishment of the future judgment.

Romans 13:5 Therefore be subject, not only because of wrath, but also because of conscience. 

Paul rightly says that they ought to be subject not only because of wrath, that is, present vengeance, for wrath produces retribution, but also because of the future judgment; for if they escape here, punishment awaits them there, where they themselves will be punished by their accusing conscience.

Romans 13:6 For this reason you pay taxes; for they are God’s ministers, serving for this very purpose. 

Therefore Paul says that taxes, or what are called monetary dues, are to be paid, so that they may show subjection, by which they may know that they are not free, but act under a power that is from God; for they are subject to their own ruler, who acts in God’s place, just as they are subject to God, as the prophet Daniel says: “For the kingdom is God’s, and to whomever He wills, He will give it” (Dan. 4:14). Therefore also the Lord says: “Render to Caesar the things that are Caesar’s” (Matt. 22:21). Therefore they are to be subject to him as to God, and the proof of this subjection is that they pay him taxes.

Romans 13:7a Render therefore to all their dues. 

Paul wishes debts to be paid by all, because even the powerful are debtors to their inferiors, so that they may respond to their merits.

Romans 13:7b To whom tribute is due, tribute; to whom tax is due, tax. 

First Paul commands that those things owed to royal authority be paid, because in these matters there is greater reason or necessity.

Romans 13:7c To whom fear is due, fear. 

Fear is to be shown to authority, because fear prevents sin; then to a parent or earthly master, so that he may give thanks for the Christian son or servant.

Romans 13:7d To whom honor is due, honor. 

This honor may also be shown toward those who seem lofty in the world, so that, seeing the humility of Christ’s servants, they may praise rather than condemn evangelical discipline.

Romans 13:8a Owe no one anything, except to love one another. 

Paul wants us to have peace, if possible, with everyone, but love with our brothers, and even this with honor preserved. He calls them debtors because it is fitting and owed to bow before a person worthy of honor, whether present or future. For one bows to him from whom he has received honor; therefore he is called a debtor. For if you do not do this to your prince, you are proud; for a person must be honored either for merit or for age.

Romans 13:8b For whoever loves his neighbor has fulfilled the Law. 

He fulfills the Law given through Moses who loves his neighbor; for the commandment of the new law is also to love enemies.

Romans 13:9a For, “You shall not commit adultery, you shall not kill, you shall not steal, you shall not covet” (see Matt. 5:43).

Moses received this written from God for the restoration of the natural law.

Romans 13:9b And if there is any other commandment, it is summed up in this saying: “You shall love your neighbor as yourself”; 

This is written in Leviticus (Lev. 19:5). Since, therefore, the commandments mentioned above remain, Paul signifies that the Law is fulfilled in love, as he said: even if there are other commandments that he has now left unmentioned, love nevertheless satisfies all the commandments. For if the human race had loved one another from the beginning, there would be no iniquity on earth; for the origin of unrighteousness is discord. Therefore, love of iniquity is itself iniquity; because what is evil to the wicked is good to the good.

Romans 13:10 Love of neighbor does no evil; for love is the fulfillment of the Law. 

It does no evil because love is good, and that which is the perfection of the Law cannot sin. Through the words of the Law, Paul wishes to arrive at the evangelical meaning. For this reason he recalls what is greatest in the Law, so that he may join the Gospel to it, showing that they have one author and one meaning. But because something needed to be added in Christ’s time, he commanded that not only neighbors but also enemies be loved. Therefore it is added: The fulfillment of the Law is love (Matt. 5:43): so that it is righteous to love one’s neighbor, but abundant and perfect righteousness also to love one’s enemies. But what else is it to love an enemy than to wish that he cease from hatred and to seek nothing harsh against him? It is to love him by wishing for him those things through which he may have God’s favor: this is heavenly righteousness, which makes us like God the Father, who bestows annual gifts even on those who do not worship him. For the Lord, too, while hanging on the cross, intercedes for his enemies (Luke 23:35), so that he might demonstrate the fullness of the righteousness he had taught.

Romans 13:11a And knowing this time, that it is already the hour for us to rise from sleep. 

Paul says that it is time for us to advance toward merit. That is, to rise from sleep and do good, as in the day, that is, openly; for illegal things are done at night, that is, in secret. For now, having been placed in the light of revealing, which is in the knowledge of God, and recognizing what we ought to follow, we must strive to live purely and attain the promised reward, having shaken off sleep, that is, ignorance or negligence.

Romans 13:11b For our salvation is now nearer than when we believed. 

It is clear that, after baptism, those who live well and devote themselves to love are not far from the merit of the promised resurrection. For the good life of a Christian is a sign of future salvation.

Romans 13:12a The night has passed, but the day has drawn near. 

Paul signified by the night the old man, who has been renewed through baptism. He says that it has passed like the night, while the day has drawn near, by whose light the truth has appeared to us, so that we might know what we ought to do. For previously, not knowing Christ, we were in darkness; but when we learned of him, light arose within us; for we passed from falsehood to truth.

Romans 13:12b Let us therefore cast off the works of darkness and put on the armor of light. 

The works of darkness are a carnal life, driven by worldly enticements; this is deserving of darkness, as the Lord says: Take him away; and, binding his hands and feet, cast him into the outer darkness (Matt. 22:13). But putting on the armor of light consists in performing good deeds; for just as evil acts are assigned to darkness, because they are done secretly by those who do evil, so those who do good act openly, because they are not afraid but rejoice. Good deeds, therefore, are the armor of light, waging war against darkness, which is the vices of the flesh.

Romans 13:13a Let us walk honestly, as in the day. 

It is true; since one does not sin publicly, let us act as one acts publicly; for nothing is so public as truth.

Romans 13:13b Not in festivities and drunkenness. 

Festivities are luxurious banquets, which are either celebrated by contributions from all, or customarily hosted in turn by companions; so that no one there is ashamed to say or do anything dishonorable, because each person assumes that it is his own banquet. For at another’s table they are usually restrained by shame; but here they gather for the purpose of doing shameful things freely, with an abundance of wine, and of arousing various pleasures of lust. Therefore Paul commands that such banquets be avoided.

Romans 13:13c Not in bedrooms and immodest acts. 

After the luxurious banquet and drunkenness Paul added what follows, namely, immodest intercourse; for this is the fruit of such wantonness.

Romans 13:13d Not in strife and rivalry. 

Paul rightly urges restraint from these, because every dispute and jealousy produces enmity, which he calls darkness; for they cannot attain the reward of light.

Romans 13:13e But put on the Lord Jesus Christ, and make no provision for the flesh in its lusts. 

Paul forbids care for the flesh, that is, pleasure; so that whatever is forbidden by the Law may not be desired, or at least, if desired, may be overcome. These are indeed the works of the flesh; having cast them off, let them put on the Lord Jesus Christ, that is, renewed through Christ, let them refrain from these evils. For he has put on Christ who has separated himself from every error and disgrace, lest he be found without a new garment at the wedding feast and be shamefully cast out and consigned to darkness (Matt. 22:11); otherwise, they have not put on Jesus Christ the Lord, over whose new garment they put on old rags; for having put off the old man, they must remain in newness of life.

Chapter 14

Romans 14:1 Welcome the one who is weak in faith, not for disputes over opinions. 

Since there were Jews who had brought the Romans to the name of Christ, with the Law mixed in, as I mentioned at the beginning of the epistle, some therefore thought that they ought not to eat forbidden meat; while others who followed Christ without the Law thought that, contrary to the Law, it was permitted to eat. Therefore disputes arose among them, which the Apostle, seeking unity and salvation, removes through divine reasoning, showing that neither does eating benefit one before God, nor does not eating harm one. He calls weak the one who fears to eat because the Jews were forbidden to do so. He therefore wishes such a person to be left to his own judgment, lest, having suffered scandal, he withdraw in mental disturbance from love, which is, as it were, the mother of souls; for Christians ought to be peaceful and calm.

Romans 14:2a For another believes that everything should be eaten. 

This confidence in reading does not hesitate to eat everything that was given for human use. For Paul reads in Genesis that everything God created was very good (Gen. 1:31); therefore nothing should be rejected, since neither Enoch, who was the first to please God, nor Noah, who alone was found righteous in the flood, nor Abraham, that friend of God, nor Isaac, nor Jacob, righteous men and friends of God, among whom was Lot, nor the other righteous are read to have abstained from these things.

Romans 14:2b But he who is weak should eat vegetables. 

Therefore, since he thinks that he should eat vegetables, he must not be persuaded to eat meat, lest he eat with a doubt and seem to sin because he does not keep his resolve.

Romans 14:3a Therefore, let the one who eats not despise the one who does not eat; and let the one who does not eat not judge the one who eats. 

For eating or not eating is a matter of choice; and therefore no dispute should arise from this. For all things were made for this purpose: that they should be subject to human will, because they are subject to human dominion.

Romans 14:3b For God has accepted him. 

He was accepted by God when he was called to grace.

Romans 14:4a Who are you to judge another’s servant? He stands or falls before his own master. 

It is clear that a servant ought not to judge the will of a fellow servant, to whom the law has not been handed down in this matter; for God is the judge, whose servant he is, of the conscience with which he eats or does not eat.

Romans 14:4b And he will stand, for God is able to make him stand.

He will stand, Paul says, before his master, because whether he eats, he will not be liable; nor, if he does not eat, will he be blameworthy, provided that he does this out of devotion, not avoiding it as though it were contrary.

Romans 14:5a For one person judges one day differently from another: 

That is, someone prefers to eat on alternate days. For there are some who have decided that meat should not be eaten on Wednesdays; there are those who abstain on Saturdays; and again there are those who eat from Easter until Pentecost.

Romans 14:5b But another judges every day. 

He judges every day who never eats.

Romans 14:5c Let each be fully convinced in his own mind: 

That is, let each be left to his own judgment.

Romans 14:6a Whoever observes the day, observes it for the Lord. 

This is true, because whoever always abstains thinks that he pleases God.

Romans 14:6b And whoever eats, eats for the Lord. 

He eats for the Lord because he gives thanks to the Creator. And whoever does not eat, does not eat for the Lord, and gives thanks to God. He gives thanks while acknowledging that the creature belongs to God and was created good, but that he abstains from it, not condemning it.

Romans 14:7 For none of us lives for himself, and no one dies for himself. 

Someone would live for himself if he did not act under the Law; but whoever is governed by the bridle of the Law certainly does not live for himself, but for God who gave the Law, so that he may live according to His will. And whoever dies likewise dies for God, by whose judgment he will either be crowned or condemned.

Romans 14:8 For whether we live, we live to the Lord; whether we die, we die to the Lord. 

The meaning is the same. Therefore, whether we live or die, we belong to the Lord. It is true that we all belong to the Lord as servants by condition and under the dominion of the Redeemer, and that each person is treated according to his merit.

Romans 14:9 For Christ died and rose again for this purpose, that He might be Lord both of the dead and of the living.

Through Christ the creature was made, which, having been alienated from its Creator through sin, was taken captive; God the Father, lest His work should perish, sent His Son from heaven to earth to teach it how, by what it did, it might escape the hands of pirates. For this reason He also allowed Himself to be killed by His enemies, so that, descending into Hades [inferos], because He had been killed innocent, He might condemn sin and release those whom it held in Hades [inferos]. Therefore, since He showed the living the way of salvation and offered Himself for them, while He freed the dead from Hades [inferno], He is Lord of both the living and the dead; for from those who were lost He converted them again for Himself as servants.

Romans 14:10 But why do you judge your brother for not eating? Or why do you despise your brother for eating? For we shall all stand before the judgment seat of Christ. 

Paul teaches that one ought not to judge in this matter, both because it is not contained in the Law and because we await God as judge.

Romans 14:11 For it is written: “As I live, says the Lord, every knee shall bow before me, and every tongue shall confess to God” (Isaiah 45:24).

This is written in Isaiah, because every tongue will confess to God in the faith of Christ. For since the one who was killed rose again and will be the judge, he rightly says: “As I live, says the Lord”; and not only do I live, but I will also judge, and my enemies will be struck with astonishment at me and will bend the knee, recognizing me as God from God.

Romans 14:12 So each of us will give an account of himself to God. 

For, Paul says, since we will each give an account for ourselves, let us not condemn one another in this matter written above.

Romans 14:13a Therefore, let us no longer judge one another;

 That is, knowing this, let us cease from discord.

Romans 14:13b Rather, judge this instead: that you do not place a stumbling block or cause of offense before your brother. 

He urges judgment in matters that are beneficial and can be defended by the authority of the law: let no offense be caused to any of the brothers, whether eating meat or not eating it.

Romans 14:14a I know and trust in the Lord that nothing is unclean through him. 

It reveals that, by the grace of the Savior, all things are clean; for, having rescued people from under the yoke of the Law and justified them, he restored the condition of their former freedom, so that they may rightly use the whole of creation, just as the holy people of old did. But those who are still under the Law are not permitted to use or eat what the Law forbids, because they despise the indulgence granted. For they are not unclean according to their nature, but become so when eaten contrary to the decree. Finally, in the Law he says: “They shall be unclean for you,” etc. (Lev. 11:4).

Romans 14:14b And plainly, to one who considers something unclean, it is unclean. 

Whether someone who is a Christian from among the Jews, or a faithful Gentile, thinks that something of this kind must be avoided, he is the one whom he above calls weak; for he is weak through doubt. To such a person, what he thinks should not be eaten is unclean; and since he does this not out of superstition but out of fear, he should be left to his own judgment.

Romans 14:15a For if your brother is distressed because of food, you are no longer walking according to love. 

In another epistle Paul says: “Food is for the stomach, and the stomach for food; but God will destroy both the one and the other” (1 Cor. 6:13). Since no one is either pleasing or displeasing to God because of food, he therefore urges us to pursue love, by which God has agreed to deliver us; for he says: “But God, being rich in mercy, because of his great love with which he loved us” (Eph. 2:4). Therefore, whoever remembers this benefit cultivates love and places nothing before it; but he avoids those great things which he knows do not secure a promise with God.

Romans 14:15b Do not destroy by your food the one for whom Christ died. 

How precious the salvation of a brother is can be understood from Christ’s death. Therefore, whoever knows its cost ought to strengthen him, not scandalize him, so as to make him doubtful over a trivial matter; and he may begin to hesitate whether meat should be eaten or not, though perhaps he had been eating it simply, secure in his conscience. But when controversies arise, he will begin, through his worry, to sin against God’s creature, and it will become an insult to the Creator, leading to the destruction of the one who doubts.

Romans 14:16 Therefore, let not our good be blasphemed. 

That is, since the Lord’s teaching is good and useful, it must not be blasphemed on account of something frivolous; but it is blasphemed when there is doubt concerning God’s creature. Our good may also be understood to be blasphemed in this way: whoever has good works, if he is criticized for some other minor matter, obscures that good of his, and his good will begin to be blasphemed because of his evil, as it is written in Ezekiel: “The righteousness of the righteous shall not profit him, if he has erred” (Ezek. 33:12). It is like someone who, though otherwise handsome, has some defect on his forehead, or a flattened nose, whereby his appearance is disfigured. Therefore, he urges that those things be pursued which do not deprive one’s other good qualities of approval.

Romans 14:17 For the kingdom of God is not food and drink. 

It is clear that no one pleases or displeases God on account of food. But it is righteousness, and peace, and joy in the Holy Spirit. Paul says that those enter the kingdom of God who pursue righteousness, while possessing the Christian peace which the Lord gave, saying: “My peace I give you, my peace I leave you” (Jn. 14:27); from this arises joy in the Holy Spirit. Dispute, however, does not produce joy, but anger; therefore the Holy Spirit does not look upon it there, because He rejoices only in those who make peace. For just as He is saddened by us, so also He rejoices in us.

Romans 14:18 For whoever serves Christ in this is pleasing to God and approved by men. 

Since Christ redeemed us, therefore Paul says: Whoever serves Christ in this, as Redeemer, is pleasing to God. Why? Because he himself sent Christ to redeem the human race, just as the Lord himself says: Whoever does not honor the Son does not honor the Father who sent him (Jn. 5:23). Whoever, therefore, is pleasing to God is approved by men. How? For he has received a gift by which he may appear worthy of God.

Romans 14:19 Therefore, let us pursue the things that make for peace and preserve one another’s edification. 

Since disputation produces discord, and so that we may be able to be peaceable, Paul teaches us to conceal our intention of eating or not eating. Moreover, he urges us to follow the way of edification, so that we may combine these things with peace, by which we may build one another up, avoiding those things that are unfruitful and more opposed. For discussion can be beneficial, since it stimulates the mind, provided that the desire to win is rejected; for this is what produces strife.

Romans 14:20a Do not destroy the work of God for the sake of food.

Man is the work of God by creation, and again the work of God while being reformed through regeneration. Food too is the work of God, but man is not for food; rather, food is for man: therefore there is a great difference. Therefore Paul says, “Do not destroy this work of God, which is excellent, for the sake of that which is most insignificant.” This is to care for the salvation of one’s brother, not to concern oneself with food so as to grieve one’s brother. For he renders God’s benefit ineffective who, through conflict, again provokes a brother freed from sin to sin, undoing in him the works of Christ, which He accomplished to free man from sin.

Romans 14:20b Indeed, all things are clean. 

It is true and evident that all things are clean, since in Genesis we read that everything God made was very good; but it is evil for the person who eats through causing offense (Gen. 1:31). Therefore, although by nature all things are good and clean, they become unclean to those who doubt; and it will be a stumbling block to the one who eats while doubting, with an unclean conscience, because he does what he considers useless for himself. Therefore no one should be contradicted regarding what Paul follows in this matter.

Romans 14:21a It is good not to eat meat and not to drink wine. 

When Paul was speaking only about meat, he added drink as well, so as to encourage those who abstain from these things by pointing out that it is permitted both to eat meat and to drink wine. So that they would not be saddened by those who use them, he gave them the comfort of relief, so that they might rest in their own choice and the dispute that causes disharmony might cease. For when they learn that it is clearly good to eat meat and drink wine, and conversely that it is good not to eat meat and not to drink wine, no one quarrels. For creation was given for the use of those who wish to use it; nevertheless, no necessity has been imposed on either the willing or the unwilling.

Romans 14:21b Nor should you do anything by which your brother is offended, scandalized, or weakened. 

Paul likewise says that the brother who has made up his mind to eat because of his weak conscience should not be subjected to questioning, lest, being troubled, he take offense, not knowing what position to hold.

Romans 14:22a You have faith to yourself: have it before God. 

That is, you who sit secure, because God’s creature is good; there is no need for you to judge another, since it is better that you have peace with your brother; for this is useful before God. In food there is benefit for the flesh, but in peace there is benefit for both flesh and soul; and therefore one must cease from such conflict, so that each person may remain in the purpose of his heart.

Romans 14:22b Blessed is the one who does not condemn himself in what he approves. 

He judges each person worthy of condemnation who does what he says he ought not to do; and he calls blessed the one who does nothing except what he proves useful for himself.

Romans 14:23a But whoever doubts is condemned if he eats.

This is true, because whoever judges that it ought not to be eaten, and eats it, is condemned; for he condemns himself when he does what he declares to be useless for himself. Because it is not from faith. He certainly does not eat from faith who denies that it ought to be eaten, and yet eats.

Romans 14:23b But whatever is not from faith is sin. 

Paul rightly calls sin that which is done otherwise than it has been approved. Since the Romans had been brought up under the law, as I mentioned at the beginning of the epistle: when those who believed more correctly arrived, questions arose concerning eating meat and not eating it; and the party that said it should be eaten and not disapproved seemed better, because all things are very clean. He calls those weak who denied that it should be eaten, whether they were Jews or Gentiles, and leaves them to the purpose of their own heart. For it neither harms one not to eat, nor benefits one before God to eat; lest, being bound in any way by what they nevertheless ate with doubt, they should sin.

Chapter 15

Romans 15:1 We who are stronger ought to bear the infirmities of the weaker. 

Paul rightly says, “We ought”; for teachers must strengthen the weak and instruct the unlearned with gentleness, lest, provoked while striving to contend and appear not humble, they should advance toward worse things.

Romans 15:2a And not to please ourselves,

That is, not to claim what benefits and pleases us, but also what benefits our brother; for we ought to care for one another.

Romans 15:2b Let each of us please his neighbor for his good, to build him up.

Now Paul has distinguished his own person; for he admonishes these people, as they strive for love, to please their neighbors for what is useful. This is edification, as he says elsewhere: “I please all people in all things” (1 Cor. 10:13).

Romans 15:3 For Christ also did not please himself, but, as it is written: “The accusations of those who accused you have fallen upon me.” 

Paul does not say that the Savior pleased himself, but God the Father; for he says: “For I have not come down from heaven to do my own will, but the will of him who sent me, the Father” (Jn. 6:38). And because, when he said these things, he was killed by the Jews who contradicted him as though he were a sinner, the Psalmist, speaking in his person, addresses these words to God the Father: “The accusations of those who accused you have fallen upon me” (Ps. 68:10); that is: While I was doing your will, they said that I was sinning against you; by not receiving me, whom you sent, they accused you. For when the Jews sinned against God by not receiving Christ, whom he sent, they even killed him as though he were sinning against God. Therefore the sins of those who sinned against God fell upon Christ; for the innocent is killed by sinners as a blasphemer, as it is written in the Gospel (Mark 15:28).

Romans 15:4 For whatever things were written were written for our confirmation, so that through the patience and encouragement of the Scriptures we might have hope. 

It is clear that whatever things were written were written for our instruction, so that through this very encouragement we may advance toward hope, not distrusting the promises, even if they are still delayed.

Romans 15:5-6 Now may the God of patience and comfort grant you to have the same understanding toward one another according to Jesus Christ, so that with one mind and one voice you may honor God and the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ. 

The Apostle, sent as though for salvation, follows the people with a good wish, desiring that God grant them one understanding of wisdom according to Jesus Christ, so that they may think in accordance with Christ’s teaching. Then they will be able to preserve love, following the example of the Lord who said: “No one has greater love than this, that he lay down his life for his friends and brothers” (Jn. 15:13); and therefore, with one voice and one confession, praise God the Father in Christ, if they encourage one another according to the mind of Christ, so that all may praise God without ceasing for everything he has done through Christ, and for having consented, after their fall through error, to raise them up and restore them again through the same one, granting a twofold benefit: mercy and knowledge.

Romans 15:7 Therefore, accept one another, just as Christ also accepted you, for the honor of God (Isa. 56:4).

Therefore, we have been accepted by Christ, when he took upon himself our weaknesses and bore our illnesses; so that, by this example, we may strengthen one another in our weaknesses through patience, lest the name of the honor of God assumed by us be rendered void; for we are called children of God through the grace of Christ.

Romans 15:8 For I say that Christ Jesus was a minister of the circumcision for the truth of God, in order to confirm the promises made to the Fathers. 

Paul praises the origin of the Jews; for in circumcision he signifies the sons of Abraham, to whom Christ, having been sent, ministered the grace promised to the Fathers. Therefore the Savior says: “For I am,” he says, “in the midst of you” (Luke 22:27), not to be ministered to, but to minister; so that the truth in the promise made to the Fathers might be demonstrated. For circumcision of the flesh was given to Abraham as a figure of circumcision of the heart, which the prophet later signified as future, saying: “Circumcise the hardness of your hearts” (Jer. 4:4). Christ is the minister of this promised circumcision through his words, that is, its preacher. Then he said to the apostles, so that they might minister circumcision of the heart to those circumcised in the flesh: “As you sent me into this world, so I also sent them into this world” (Jn. 6:58). Circumcision of the heart, however, is to recognize God the Father as Creator, and his Son Christ, through whom he created all things, once the cloud of error has been removed, so that the truth of God might be fulfilled. For he had promised that he would grant mercy, and had promised it to the fathers of the Jews; for he had said to Abraham: “In your seed all nations shall be blessed” (Gen. 22:18). And to David he said: “I will set upon your throne one from the fruit of your womb” (Ps. 131:11), and: “A star shall rise from Jacob” (Num. 24:17).

Romans 15:9a But the Gentiles honor God because of mercy. 

Since no promise had been made to them, they were taken up to salvation, as unworthy, through mercy alone, so that by their confession they might honor God; for unbelievers dishonor him.

Romans 15:9b As it is written: “Therefore I will confess to you among the Gentiles, and sing to your name.” 

Paul proves this by prophetic example; for it is written in the seventeenth psalm that the Gentiles were to be admitted to the grace of God to receive salvation (Ps. 17:50). For this is the voice of Christ, who foretold that his preaching among the Gentiles would bear the fruit of confessing the mysetery of God; therefore the Son gives thanks to the Father for the obedience of the Gentiles. Therefore he says in the Gospel: “I will confess to you, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because you have hidden these things from the wise and prudent, and revealed them to little ones; for so it was well pleasing before you” (Matt. 11:25). This confession, therefore, is of the one God in the Trinity, from which joy arises; so that, after confessing the truth, he may joyfully sing of the mercy and gift of God.

Romans 15:10-11 And again he says: “Rejoice, nations, with his people,” in this song of Deuteronomy (Deut. 32:43). And again: “Praise the Lord, all nations, and magnify him, all peoples.” (Ps. 116:1)

In the one hundred and sixteenth Psalm he shows that God had long ago decreed, through his mercy, to unite Jews and nations in harmony, so that the Gentiles, having obtained grace, would become companions of the Jews, who themselves had once been called his people by God’s gift. But now the Jews are honorable; these others, formerly dishonorable, have been dignified through mercy, so that all may rejoice together in the knowledge of the truth; and while the nations praise God, all peoples may magnify him, the twelve tribes worshipping one God, who increased the number of his people by adding the nations. Finally, when the Jews disputed with the apostle Peter concerning Cornelius, after he gave them his explanation, they agreed, magnifying the Lord and saying: “Perhaps God has also granted repentance to the Gentiles unto the attainment of life” (Acts 11:18).

Romans 15:12 And again Isaiah says: “There shall be a root of Jesse; and he who rises to rule the nations, in him the nations shall hope” (Isa. 11:10).

To give the nations greater confidence and an unquestionable hope, Paul confirms through many testimonies that it was God’s decree that all nations should be blessed in Christ; lest the disrespect of the unbelieving Jews should grieve them and make the souls of the believing nations doubtful, as though they were promising themselves hope in vain, since the faith of the Jews was credited to the God of Abraham, so that they might grow in joy and security. Why, however, is Christ from the root of Jesse, and not from the root of Boaz, the righteous man, or from the root of Obed? Because he is called the son of David on account of the kingdom; so that, just as he was born from God to be king, he would also have his origin in the flesh from David the king. Therefore the root of Jesse is the tree of David, which produced fruit through the branch that is Mary the Virgin, who bore Christ.

Romans 15:13-14 Now may the God of hope fill you with all joy and peace in believing, so that you may abound in hope through the power of the Holy Spirit. I am convinced, my brothers, and I myself am convinced about you, that you too are full of love, filled with all knowledge, so that you are able to reprove one another. 

These are words of exhortation; for by praising them Paul urges them toward better understanding and life. For whoever sees himself praised works diligently to make true the things that are said. Therefore he did not say that they should teach one another, but reprove one another; for one is usually reproved about something that, though known, sometimes slips from the mind. The rest is not obscure, so as to require explanation.

Romans 15:15-16 I have written to you rather boldly, brothers, in part, as reminding you, because of the grace given to me by God; to be a minister of Christ Jesus among the Gentiles, sanctifying the Gospel of God, so that the offering of the Gentiles may be acceptable and sanctified in the Holy Spirit. 

For Paul does not imply that he wrote rashly, but, saying that authority was given to him through God’s grace, he dares to write to all the Gentiles, reproving and confirming their purpose in Christ; so that he may show his concern in the ministry of the Gospel, as a teacher of the Gentiles, and that their sacrifice may become acceptable for the sake of sanctification in the Holy Spirit. For whatever is offered with complete faith and a sober mind is purified by the Holy Spirit.

Romans 15:17-19 Therefore I have glory in Christ before God; for I dare not speak of anything that Christ has not accomplished through me for the obedience of the Gentiles in word and deed, by the power of signs and wonders, through the might of the Holy Spirit.
